**Praise for *The Definitive Guide to Blockchain for Accounting and Business***

## FOREWORD

Saurav Dutta’s *The Definitive Guide to Blockchain for Accounting and Business: Understanding the Revolutionary Technology* is a timely tour de force for blockchain business understanding. It’s crisply written with clear concise non-technical explanations of the key concepts that business professionals need to understand around the technology. But it’s not a technical primer, rather it stands out as a seminal work in bridging the gap between technology and business. By first building or solidifying the reader’s understanding of the core foundational concepts of blockchain and distributed ledgers, Saurav provides a graceful onramp into an exploration of the implications for business and accounting professionals. Through the highlighting of emerging business models built on these new technologies, then exploring the implications for careers in accounting and internal controls, the reader is provided an unbroken view of the whole region surrounding and supporting the emergence of one of the most important technologies of the twenty-first century.

David Deputy

President, Accounting Blockchain Coalition

Director, Strategic Development and Emerging Markets

Vertex, Inc. (U.S.A.) 

 

## ABOUT THE AUTHOR

![image](images/img_001.jpg)

Professor Saurav Kumar Dutta is the Head of School of Accounting at Curtin University, Australia. He previously was the Chairman of the Department of Accounting and Business Law at the State University of New York and has held faculty appointments at Rutgers University (New Jersey, U.S.A.) and the City University of New York. He holds a Bachelor of Technology from the Indian Institute of Technology, Bombay (India), and a Ph. D. from the University of Kansas (U.S.A.). He was awarded the 1989 Robert Beyer Silver Medal for securing the second highest total score in the CMA examination and was also the recipient of the National Talent Search Scholarship awarded by the Government of India.

Throughout his academic career, spanning over 25 years, he has engaged in high-impact research which has greatly informed industry practices. He has influenced public policy in his role as an Academic Fellow in the Office of Chief Accountant at the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC). In that role, he helped implement the Dodd-Frank Act and other measures from ‘lessons learned’ during the 2008 global financial crisis. While at the SEC, Saurav also served as a liaison with FASB, PCAOB, IASB and IAASB, all of which are critical standard-setting bodies that affect the accounting profession.

Saurav has been engaged by the New York State Attorney General’s Office to investigate billing practices for a multi-national wholesale distributor. He was also engaged by the SEC to determine appropriateness of derivative accounting of a Fortune 50 insurance company and treatment of consolidation accounting of a Fortune 500 company resulting in settlement and imposition of monetary fines on the companies. Under the purview of the United States District Court of the Southern District of New York, he was involved in designing statistical procedures to aid in the disbursement of amounts ranging between $500 million and $6.1 billion for settlements of MCI-WorldCom, Global Crossing, Cendant, and HealthSouth. He was also involved with the reparations of more than 400 million Swiss Francs from the Swiss banks to Holocaust victims, under the purview of the U.S. District Court of Eastern District of New York. Recently, he was engaged by Apple Computers as a defense expert in $600 million lawsuit under the jurisdiction of the U.S. District Court in the Northern District of California.

Saurav has authored a book, *Statistical Techniques for Forensic Accounting*, published by Financial Times Press in 2013. He has also authored White Papers and Course material for the Institute of Management Accounting. He has published more than 40 academic papers in reputed U.S. and international journals and has presented his work in numerous national and international venues.

## 1

## INTRODUCTION

A decade back, the words cryptocurrency, Bitcoin, blockchain, etc., were relatively unknown; however, over the past decade, cryptocurrency has gained popularity in both academia and popular culture. As one of the most successful cryptocurrency, Bitcoin’s market capitalization in early 2020 was around $120 billion. It had reached a maximum of $237 billion in the fourth quarter of 2017, up from $70 billion the previous quarter. The almost 300% gain in value over that quarter didn’t go unnoticed and public interest in cryptocurrency and its technology grew. Since then, in about two years,[¹](#CH001.html#fn1) the number of blockchain wallet owners increased from less than 17 million in third quarter 2017 to around 45 million worldwide.

With increased popularity of Bitcoin, blockchain the infrastructure supporting Bitcoin also received extensive attention. The features of blockchain in maintaining detailed and trusted records were recognized to have many useful features. Over the past few years, 2017 onwards, blockchain-based applications are proliferating in many industries, and not just in financial industry. While there still remain challenges to be overcome, blockchain’s potential currently seems unbounded, as evidenced by increased worldwide investment in its development.

Worldwide, the spending on blockchain solutions almost doubled from $1.5 billion in 2018 to $2.9 billion in 2019. The spending on blockchain development is expected to continue to grow at the same pace, projected to be about $15.9 billion by 2023 (see [Fig. 1.1](#CH001.html#fig1)).[²](#CH001.html#fn2) The financial sector leads the spending in blockchain solutions by investing more than $1.1 billion in its development, followed by the manufacturing sector and the distribution sector, each investing upwards of $600 million. The United States had the largest blockchain spending at $1.1 billion, followed by Western Europe and China. In October 2019, President Xi Jinping gave a speech saying China needs to “seize the opportunities” presented by blockchain, in what appeared to be one of the first instances of a major world leader backing the technology. Xi said blockchain is an “important breakthrough in independent innovation of core technologies.”[⁴](#CH001.html#fn4)

**Fig. 1.1:    Projected Worldwide Investment in Blockchain (Billions of US$).[³](#CH001.html#fn3)**

![image](images/C01-FIG1.jpg)

This great interest and large investment in blockchain technology is not without prospects. According to a leading research firm, Gartner, “by 2025, the business value added by blockchain will grow to slightly more than $176 billion, then surge to exceed $3.1 trillion by 2030.”[⁵](#CH001.html#fn5) Reflecting the same sentiment, more than half of business leaders surveyed by Deloitte in 2019 placed blockchain in the five most critical strategic priorities and an additional 27% noted that it was important though not critical.[⁶](#CH001.html#fn6) In the same survey, over 80% of respondents believed there was a compelling business case for blockchain and that it will eventually be adopted by mainstream businesses.

Considering the above trend, it is imperative for business leaders and professionals to obtain a non-technical understanding of blockchain, how it works, what are its advantages and limitations, what are the security and privacy issues and the financial and regulatory hindrances, if any. This book is written to provide a non-technical explanation of the underlying technology and the features of blockchain for accounting and business professionals.

Blockchain is a recordkeeping system in which events termed as transactions are stored in a *block*. The blocks are appended to previous blocks, forming a *chain*. The uniqueness of the technology is that it ensures the way the blocks are added forms a *chain* and not a *tree*, in that there are no *forks* or major *branches*. The record or *ledger* is maintained at multiple locations and no single entity has control over the records, giving it the feature of being *distributed*. The mathematical discipline of *cryptography* and computer technique of *hash function* is widely used to help code the data for easier storage. The key characteristics of blockchain are decentralization, persistency, anonymity and auditability. One of the key advantages of blockchain is that it facilitates monetary exchanges between untrusting strangers without the services of a financial intermediary or a “trusted third party.” This feature enabled transactions in cryptocurrencies and transfer of digital assets between unknown and untrusting parties.

One of the key features of blockchain is its immutability, which implies that when records are inscribed on blockchain those could not be erased or altered. Business functions or processes that require high reliability and honesty are therefore prime beneficiaries of blockchain adoption. Moreover, business processes that incur immense resources in cumbersome recordkeeping where authenticity of records is suspect, blockchain provides a solution.

Though gaining recognition, as of past decade, some computer science theories and algorithms that underwrite the functionalities of blockchain were proposed decades ago. [Fig. 1.2](#CH001.html#fig2) provides a timeline of the introduction of various computer science developments that support the functioning of blockchain.

A fundamental part of blockchain technology is what is known as the Merkle trees. Merkle tree is a structure that allows for efficient and secure verification of content in a large body of data. This tree provides blockchain the key property of auditability and verifiability. The concept of Merkle tree was developed by Ralph Merkle (1979) as his Ph.D. dissertation at Stanford University, USA. Merkle trees will be further discussed in [Chapter 3](#CH003.html). Independent of Merkle trees and research on data storage techniques, Nick Szabo (1994) proposed a concept which later came to be known as the “smart contract.” Szabo defined smart contracts as machine readable transaction which creates a contract with pre-determined terms.[⁷](#CH001.html#fn7) Smart contracts are discussed in detail in [Chapter 5](#CH005.html). In 1999, the term “proof-of-work” was coined in an academic journal article.[⁸](#CH001.html#fn8) Satoshi Nakamoto launched Bitcoin on January 3, 2009, and the coin gained immense popularity. The white paper published by Satoshi Nakamoto is discussed in the next chapter. Vitalik Buterin, a Canadian teenager,[⁹](#CH001.html#fn9) published a white paper that led to the launching of Ethereum in 2015. In August 2017, the market capitalization of Bitcoin surpassed $100 billion for the first time, and soon thereafter the market capitalization of Ethereum also exceeded $100 billion.[^(10)](#CH001.html#fn10)

**Fig. 1.2:    Key Event Dates in Blockchain History.**

![image](images/C01-FIG2.jpg)

The remainder of the book is organized in three parts. In Part 1, [Chapters 2](#CH002.html)–[6](#CH006.html), we develop the basics of blockchain technology for non-technical readership. We start by discussing the linkage between Bitcoin and blockchain and why the infrastructure of blockchain was essential for enabling cryptocurrency transactions. In [Chapter 3](#CH003.html), we present the terminology and nomenclature of blockchain, we provide precise definitions of the words and contrast those with the common usage of that word in accounting and business terminology. Some of the confusions and over-selling of blockchains can be attributed to coining of terminology in blockchain by using well-known terminology in accounting and business and using those in broader or different context. In [Chapter 4](#CH004.html), we explain the consensus mechanism. In [Chapters 5](#CH005.html) and [6](#CH006.html), we describe smart contracts and tokenization, respectively.

In Part 2, [Chapters 7](#CH007.html)–[11](#CH011.html), we present use cases of blockchain applications outside of cryptocurrency. These are contributed chapters to the book by experts in the field. [Chapter 7](#CH007.html) develops the framework to evaluate whether blockchain is appropriate for a business application. In [Chapter 8](#CH008.html), a live case study of blockchain-based security offering of Curzio Research is presented. It describes the process, the launch, the experience of the issuer and the advantages over traditional offering of securities. In [Chapter 9](#CH009.html), blockchain application in tracking of conflict minerals is presented. In [Chapter 10](#CH010.html), tokenization and blockchain trading of Japanese real estate is discussed. [Chapter 11](#CH011.html) provides a few additional interesting use cases of blockchain.

In Part 3, [Chapters 12](#CH012.html)–14, we present the technical implications of blockchain for business and accounting. [Chapter 12](#CH012.html) discusses how the internal control processes would be impacted by incorporation of blockchain to record business processes. [Chapter 13](#CH013.html) presents the ramifications for auditing of financials when a business engages in transactions on blockchain. Chapter 14 concludes with a discussion of opportunities, vulnerabilities and threats that the technology currently faces.

## 2

## THE INFRASTRUCTURE SUPPORTING BITCOIN: BLOCKCHAIN

### 2.1. INTRODUCTION

Blockchain technology was developed concurrently with the launching of Bitcoin, one of the earliest and arguably the most well-known cryptocurrency. Though blockchain was an essential component that facilitated the growth and popularity of Bitcoin, its potential applications are possible in domains outside of cryptocurrency. Examples of adoption of blockchain technology for other business purposes and applications are discussed in latter chapters.

Prior to delving into the intricacies of blockchain technology and its potential applications, it is important to understand its genesis – the motivation for developing the technology, its purpose and its limitations. The concept of blockchain, though not the name, was introduced in a computer science research paper.[¹](#CH002.html#fn1) This chapter discusses the accounting, economics and financial problems of introducing a new currency, Bitcoin, which the computer scientists were addressing.

E-commerce was flourishing worldwide in the 2000s, with annual growth rate of about 30% at the turn of the century.[²](#CH002.html#fn2) Despite its growth, financial transactions on the internet relied exclusively on “trusted third parties” to facilitate the transactions by processing electronic payments. These “trusted third parties” extracted a nominal commission for each transaction, adding a cost and thereby causing friction in the system. While the commission would be negligible for transactions of large monetary value, it would be disproportionate for smaller transactions, thereby inhibiting those.

Computer scientists and internet enthusiasts became interested in creating a system that will allow financial transaction between two unknown parties without the need for a financial institution to be an intermediary. PayPal was one of the earlier innovation in this space, and became the most popular third-party payment processor with an estimated 20% of all e-commerce transactions taking place through its platform. In that sense, PayPal’s business model was to become an alternative to financial institutions as a trusted third party. Hence it was not interested in developing a system that would eliminate the need for a trusted third party. Consequently, with the advent of cryptocurrency there remained a need to develop a mechanism to facilitate e-commerce without relying on financial intermediaries or trusted third parties.

The remainder of this chapter is organized as follows. In the next section we take a historical perspective on how trade evolved and the role of money in that evolution. We next discuss the importance of trust in order for trade and economy to prosper. In Section 2.4 we introduce cryptocurrency and the many challenges it encountered. The following section introduces blockchain as providing solutions to the challenges inherent in cryptocurrency. Section 2.6 illustrates how Bitcoin transactions are recorded on blockchain. Section 2.7 concludes by arguing that features of blockchain have wider applicability than merely facilitating Bitcoin transactions.

### 2.2. EVOLUTION OF TRADE – BARTER SYSTEM TO CRYPTOCURRENCY

Trade, or exchange of goods or services, between individuals or societies has been traced back to the dawn of human civilization. Trade, in some form, predates money and was integral to the formation of modern society and has evolved through the ages as illustrated in [Fig. 2.1](#CH002.html#fig1). Trade that predated introduction of currency, was accomplished through bartering. Bartering can be traced back to 6000 BC and was practiced by the Mesopotamian tribes. Essentially, bartering is trading of goods and services between two parties without using money in the transaction. In the olden days, food, spices and weapons were commonly traded and one of the valuable medium of barter was salt. In fact, Roman soldiers were paid in salt.

FIG. 2.1:    TRADE THROUGH THE AGES.

![image](images/C02-FIG1.jpg)

*Sources*: “Ancient Egypt Fresco Fragment” by Gary Lee Todd, Ph.D.[⁹](#CH002.html#fn9)

Bartering is popular in certain sectors even today. Cross-advertising for websites is primarily done through bartering. Simplicity is one of the main advantages of bartering. However, parties in a bartering transaction often spend time and effort negotiating the terms, and the trade usually is not undertaken at a fair value, but rather the perceived value to the buyer. In the seventeenth century, the settlers in America bartered glass beads, silver trinkets and copper for much needed corn and pelts. In a barter system, the exchange of items takes place between the individuals, hence there is no need for a “trusted third party.” Once traded, the sales were usually final and could not be reversed.

Modern money was introduced much later, around 600 BC in Lydia. Introduction of money, helped Lydia increase trade, making it one of the richest empires in Asia Minor in those days. Subsequently coins made of precious and semi-precious metals, of various forms and shapes, eventually converging to the circular shape that was prevalent throughout Asia and Europe over the following 1,500 years.

Paper money was introduced in China around the seventh century AD and was in widespread use around the time Marco Polo visited circa 1270 AD. In fact, the ancient rulers of China had the basic understanding of the need to control money supply and its ramifications on the economy. Interestingly, even in those days, the government was cautious of counterfeited money. Ancient Chinese money had the inscription “All counterfeiters will be decapitated.”[³](#CH002.html#fn3) The punishment was harsh because the threat of counterfeited money on the economy was grave.

In the Western world, paper money was adopted much later in the seventeenth century in Sweden. The first foray into paper money was done by the banks, generally ending in failures. The governments started issuing paper money around the 1820s and they stabilized the currency by introducing the gold standard. Introduction of credit as a currency, occurred concurrently, around the second half of seventeenth century. The expansion of English economy, around that time, was supported by the expansion of credit. Both the paper money and availability of credit encouraged economic growth in Western Europe and its expansion into the New World.

### 2.3. TRUST – FOUNDATION OF TRADE

Trade takes place on the basis of trust. In ancient days, under barter system, two people will exchange goods in a face-to-face transaction. Each party will assess the quality of the item being traded as there was little or no recourse of return. Later, with the advent of money, the exchange would be facilitated through currency, and there was no need for the two parties to find something of value with the other person to consummate the exchange.

In traditional retail trade, the exchange of money with goods would occur almost simultaneously. If the payment is made in cash, there is no need for a trusted third party to protect the interest of the buyer and seller. However, even in those trivial situations, the party with the higher risk of reneging is usually required to keep their end of the bargain before receiving. For example, when we go through a fast food drive-through, we order the food, we pay cash and soon thereafter we pick up our order. The time lag between payment of cash and getting our order is minimal. However, as you may have noticed when using the drive-through the customer always pays prior to collecting their order. That is, the task of collection of payment is always done prior to the task of fulfillment of the order. Why is this the case? Bluntly, because the merchant does not trust the customer. The merchants believe that there is a non-zero probability that some customers will speed away with their order before paying, if the tasks were reversed, that is the merchandise was given prior to collecting cash. In that case the merchant will have no recourse. If you are wondering, why does the customer pay cash prior to collecting her order? That’s because the customer has a recourse. The restaurant has a building and simply cannot run away! Now it has happened to all of us, sometimes the order isn’t properly filled and some items are missing. In those cases, as customers we do have a recourse, and we inevitably go into the restaurant to complain and get the items we paid for. Likewise, for the same reasons, many gas stations in the United States require the customer to prepay a deposit or provide their credit card prior to filling up gas.

The prior situation is completely reversed when you go to a nice sit-down restaurant. There the customer is served food prior to paying for those. Since one is sitting down within the premises of the restaurant, it is perhaps difficult and a bit embarrassing to leave without paying the bill. The restaurant knows that and hence evaluates the risk of non-payment to be low. In these situations, the restaurant is implicitly trusting the diner, to fulfill their payment obligations after they have had their meal.

In global trade, however, especially for those conducted over the internet, the buyer and seller do not meet face to face. They transact the exchange remotely, and the goods, services or money are exchanged without physical interaction between the buyer and the seller. In these situations, either party requires protection that the other party would fulfill their obligation on the trade. When two honest people cannot trust each other because they have never met, interacted or know each other, both of them need a trusted third party to act as an intermediary to facilitate the exchange. The trusted third party provides implicit assurance to the transacting parties that the terms of the trade would be upheld, and if either party is dissatisfied with the trade, they could reverse it with minimal cost. For this service, the trusted third party collects a small fee for each transaction they underwrite.

The trusted third party can provide that assurance because it has access to the records of the parties and can assure the seller that the buyer has sufficient resources to pay for the purchase. Since the seller cannot directly observe the bank accounts or wealth of the buyer, they require the service of the financial intermediary. What if the wealth of the buyer was public knowledge, which each seller could independently verify, on a real-time basis, whether or not the buyer has the resources to make the purchase, will we still need the trusted third party to act as an intermediary?

Public record of personal wealth is not a new or a modern concept. Even in ancient civilizations, without much modern comfort or trade, people still realized that they needed some measurement of wealth through which everyone can agree that one has the ability to pay and purchase goods or services, in which case the wealth transfers from buyer to the seller. One of the classic cases of development of money, that economists love, is the case of a remote, tiny island called Yap. Yap is located in the Pacific Ocean, east of Philippines and north of Papua New Guinea. Yap had no form of precious metals to convert to currency, but they found limestone deposits on another island, named Palau, hundreds of miles away. Centuries back, the Yap people carved the limestone deposits into giant wheels (see [Fig. 2.2](#CH002.html#fig2)), and they started using these discs as the representation of wealth, or money in our lingo.[⁴](#CH002.html#fn4) The stones are called Rai and the stones are considered extremely valuable in the island of Yap. Since the Rai was difficult to produce and transport, only a few were produced, which stabilized the value of Rai for a long time. The last one was produced in 1931 and since then these stones have generally retained their value in the Yap economy.

**Fig. 2.2:    The Yap Money (Rai).[^(10)](#CH002.html#fn10)**

![image](images/C02-FIG2.jpg)

The Yap people started using the stone for currency, to purchase provisions and even for dowry. However, unlike paper currency, these stones were heavy, some weighing more than a car – a bit problematic to be carrying these around to exchange. Hence, the concept of money and wealth became more abstract. When an exchange takes place in Yap, the stone isn’t moved, except everyone in the island knows that the stone has a new owner. In fact, everyone in the village knows the current owners of the stones. The noteworthy feature of the stone currency is that it could change ownership without even as much as a mark placed on the stone to indicate the exchange, and the stone wheel remains undisturbed in the former owner’s premises. Nobel Laureate Milton Friedman compared the Yap money with the gold standard.[⁵](#CH002.html#fn5)

### 2.4. CRYPTOCURRENCY – BASIC FEATURES AND CHALLENGES

A purely peer-to-peer version of electronic cash would allow online payments to be sent directly from one party to another without going through a financial institution. Unlike real cash, cryptocurrency is stored on the users’ computer or smartphone and not in their wallet. When a buyer makes a purchase through cryptocurrency, they do not physically hand the cash or any tangible object, instead commits to the resource over the internet, akin to transmitting a file.

There is a basic distinction between transferring a computer file versus transferring a physical object. Once I give you cash or another physical object, I no longer have possession of it and cannot give the same item to another individual. However, when I transfer a computer file, say a pdf of this chapter to my editor I am sending her a copy, I still have the original on my computer. Moreover, nothing restricts me from sending the same file to another individual. Extend this example to the domain of cryptocurrency. Transferring cryptocurrency is like transferring a computer file, we are sending a copy and not the original. Therefore we could send the same file to another person. This is called *double-spending*.

While in the real world with real currency, everyone insists on getting the actual currency note and no one, I know of, would accept a photocopy. Just imagine, if we were allowed to make purchases using a photocopy of a $100 bill. Each one of us would be infinitely rich!! We could buy whatever we want as long as we had a good copier. However, in the virtual world, with virtual currency, the currency transfer was a copy and not the original, hence for Bitcoin to work the inventors had to resolve the problem of double-spending. That is, even though the transaction is happening through a copy and not the original, once the Bitcoin has been spent, the buyer will not be allowed to spend it again.

Consequently, for cryptocurrency to be successful the problem of “double-spending” needed to be solved. In traditional methods, the trusted third party will maintain the records and prevent double-spending. Digital signatures provide part of the solution, but the main benefits are lost if a trusted third party is still required to prevent double-spending. The computer scientists promoting the idea of a digital currency that does not rely on a “trusted third party” had to find an alternative solution to enable all participants the ability to validate each and every transaction on a real-time basis. That is, they needed the digital form of Yap money discussed earlier. Basically, everyone in the virtual community would know the wealth everyone possesses and be aware of all the transactions that occur in the cyber-domain using Bitcoin.

The solution proposed by Nakamoto relied on a peer-to-peer distributed solution, that later came to be known as blockchain. Peer to peer is a decentralized communication model where each party has the same capabilities and any party can initiate a session. In computer lingo, peer-to-peer network allows each node to function both as a client and as a server. One of the earliest popular applications of peer-to-peer model was the music sharing application, Napster. This architecture allows for massive parallel computing environments. In a blockchain, the peer-to-peer distributed network would timestamp each and every transaction, thereby maintaining a chronological order of transactions. The validity check for transactions is delegated to multiple users and computers. Therefore, rather than relying on the honesty and integrity of the “trusted third party,” the system now relies on the honesty and objectivity of the individual nodes verifying each transaction. Nakamoto asserted that the “system is secure as long as honest nodes collectively control more CPU power than any cooperating group of attacker nodes.” We will elaborate on this concept in a subsequent chapter.

The challenge really is that we want a mechanism to enable the seller to know that the buyer has the resources (or Bitcoins) to pay for the goods. Let’s understand the problem through a simple example. Say, my wife and I are on a vacation abroad and one morning she says she is completely out of local currency and needs to convert $500 from the bank. Later that day, I want to know how much of the foreign currency she still has remaining in her purse before I make a purchase. Rather than asking her or look through her purse, which will surely upset her, I decide to recall all the purchases she made during the day and subtract that amount from $500. If I know the amount of money she had at the start of the day and have a comprehensive list of all her spending, I can infer how much she still has left with her.

Nakamoto’s solution was similar. For each and every buyer, if we know all the Bitcoins they obtained in the past, and have a comprehensive list of all the times the buyer spent the Bitcoins, we will know how much is left and whether the buyer has the amount of Bitcoins to transfer to the seller. In that sense, it is the earliest transaction that counts. Once we have undisputed record of the earliest transactions, the future attempts to double-spend does not matter. Basically, to be certain about the absence of a transaction is to be cognizant of all the transactions. In the traditional setting, it was the role of the trusted third party to provide and validate that information.

In the Bitcoin model, in the absence of the trusted third party, each and every transaction with Bitcoin has to be publicly announced. Moreover, multiple comprehensive records of all transactions have to be maintained with multiple parties, so that no one party can change the records without the knowledge and approval of the others. Since it is only the earliest transactions that are valid, hence matter, we only need a way for all the participants to agree on a singular history of the order in which the transactions were received or executed. For the seller to be assured that the payment is valid, the majority of the participants in the system must recognize this particular transaction to be the first one received to lay a claim on the existing resources of the buyer. Once this particular seller’s transaction is recognized to be the first one, the transfer of resources is permanent and cannot be altered. This feature is defined as the *immutability*. The records are secured and permanent because they are immutable, that is once recorded these cannot be subsequently altered.

### 2.5. BLOCKCHAIN INTRODUCED

Extending the above concept to the digital domain, the participants are replaced by “nodes,” denoting a computing unit. The transactions are stored in a block, and the series of blocks comprise the blockchain. In order to ensure that the node reporting the transaction is legitimate, they have to undertake a *proof of work*.[⁶](#CH002.html#fn6) This is much like how we humans have to sometimes prove to the computer that we are not robots, by identifying traffic lights in a series of pictures! Once a transaction is validated and recorded by one of the nodes it is transmitted to all the nodes in the network. Each node maintains identical copies of the validated transactions, which are called *blocks*. The nodes start working on the next set of transactions and repeat the process. Briefly, these steps are summarized as[⁷](#CH002.html#fn7):

*   New transactions are broadcast to all nodes.
    
*   Each node collects new transactions into a block.
    
*   Each node works on finding a difficult proof of work for its block.
    
*   When a node finds a proof of work, it broadcasts the block to all nodes.
    
*   Nodes accept the block only if all transactions in it are valid and not already spent.
    
*   Nodes express their acceptance of the block by working on creating the next block in the chain.
    

The last bullet is non-trivial and needs further elaboration. Suppose at a meeting, a challenging problem is being discussed. There is a lot of conversation, but finally someone makes a great suggestion, people nod their head and move on to the next issue. The conclusion of discussion for a topic and continuation to the next topic is an implicit acknowledgment of the consensus of the final statement made on the previous topic. The agreement in blockchain follows the same principle. Once the nodes accept a solution of the *proof of work* submitted by another node and have validated all the transactions in the new block sent along with the proof of work, they start working on the next block using the *hash* of the accepted block as the previous hash.[⁸](#CH002.html#fn8)

The operationalization of the digital solution begins with a timestamp server. The timestamp server takes a hash of a block of items and widely publishes the hash. The data included in the hash indicates that the transaction must have existed at the time. Moreover, since the hash is comprehensive, if a transaction is not included in the hash it implies that the transaction had not taken place at the time. In Nakamoto’s words, “each subsequent timestamp (or block) includes the previous timestamp (block), which forms a chain” – hence blockchain. Therefore, each subsequent block includes the information in the previous block thereby reinforcing all the transactions that have come before it.

When a node receives conflicting solutions from the other nodes, by default they always consider the longest chain to be the correct one and will keep working on extending it. If two nodes, *A* and *B* broadcast different versions of the next block simultaneously, some nodes may receive *A* first and some may receive *B* first. In that case, the nodes work on the first one they received, but save the other branch in case it becomes longer before the other one does. Say node *D* received *A* first but later realized that *B* got longer and was supplanted by a new block sent from *H*. Now *D* will discard the chain *A* and start working with the longer chain from *H*. That way, it is always ensured that the longer chain is the valid one and there are not too many competing chains of the same size. When there are conflicts those are resolved quickly, that is by the next iteration.

After a new block has been formed, it implies much effort has been expended to satisfy the proof of work. Hence, the block cannot be changed without redoing the work. As the blockchain grows by addition of subsequent blocks, the work to change the records not only is limited to redoing that particular block, but redoing the work of each and every subsequent block. Therefore, as new blocks are added to the blockchain, the work needed to alter an earlier block grows exponentially. This feature of blockchain helps maintain its integrity and immutability. That is, once a transaction is added to a block, and accepted by other nodes, it cannot be altered or removed. If the transaction needs to be removed for legitimate reasons, it has to be put forth as a new transaction reversing the previous one. Hence, there will be a permanent record of the transaction having initially taken place and also the record of it being subsequently reversed, which could have different implications than showing that the transaction never occurred in the first place.

In summary, Bitcoin endeavored to create a system that would allow two unknown parties to transact virtually with each other without the need of an intermediary or a trusted third party. This required an electronic payment system that would be based on cryptographic proof. To achieve that it required the network to timestamp transactions by hashing them into an ongoing chain. This forms a ledger of sort, a detailed history of transactions, which is immutable in that it cannot be altered. The longest chain not only serves as proof of the sequence of events witnessed, but also enables the system to maintain integrity.

### 2.6. ILLUSTRATION OF BITCOIN TRANSACTION ENABLED THROUGH BLOCKCHAIN

Bitcoin was the first widely adopted mechanism of transferring money without having a centralized authority or third party that records and tracks all transfers. Consequently, the process of verifying transactions and enabling transfers is more difficult than the traditional double-entry bookkeeping system of debits and credits. Further, to complicate the mechanics, Bitcoin issues new currency to its participants as an incentive for those parties to maintain its bookkeeping systems, including verifying the validity of the transactions.

**Table 2.1:     Bitcoin Activity Statistics (as of December 2019).**

 
| Total Bitcoin mined || Approximately 18.1 million |
| --- | --- |
| Market capitalization in US $ || Approximately 127.6 billion |
| Total size of all block headers and transactions || Approximately 258,000 MB |
| Average number of transactions per block || 2,200 |
| Average block size || 1.1 MB |
| Median time for a transaction to be accepted || 9 Minutes and 20 seconds |
| Level of difficulty || 14,776, 367,535,688[^a](#CH002.html#ft1) |
| Cost per transaction || $43 |

*Source*: Data obtained from [https://www.blockchain.com/charts](https://www.blockchain.com/charts).

[^a](#CH002.html#ift1)The level of difficulty at inception throughout 2009 was 1.0 and in December 2016 was 310,153,855,703.

The growth in Bitcoin has been phenomenal. Introduced only recently in 2009, in about 10 years, the market value of Bitcoin has grown to approximately $128 billion at the end of 2019 (See [Table 2.1](#CH002.html#tab1)). To put the amount in perspective, [Fig. 2.3](#CH002.html#fig3) shows the comparable value relative to other measures.

The Bitcoin software can be downloaded for free at [https://bitcoin.org/en/choose-your-wallet](https://bitcoin.org/en/choose-your-wallet). One of the essential features of Bitcoin is the creation of a *wallet* on the user’s computer, wherein the user could store her Bitcoins. The Bitcoin software also creates an individual node for the user which is connected to Bitcoin’s peer-to-peer network and only requires a standard internet connectivity. It also provides access to the blockchain data structure and verifies all past Bitcoin activity. Regardless of when a user joins the network it obtains the entire history of Bitcoin transactions embedded in the longest chain at the time of the new user’s joining the Bitcoin network.

Possession of Bitcoins are recorded as transactions. One simply does not hold Bitcoins but participates in a publicly verifiable transaction showing how they obtained those coins. Say, I received five Bitcoins from my publisher as royalty for writing this book, of which I transferred one Bitcoin to my publishing agent, Jeanne. When Jeanne receives the Bitcoin, she verifies that I had received five Bitcoins from the publisher Emerald Publishing, and there were no prior transactions where I spent all of the five Bitcoins. In the absence of any prior transactions, between the time I received the five Bitcoins from Emerald and transferred one Bitcoin to Jeanne, the transfer to Jeanne is the first transfer and will be inscribed as such on the next block, and Jeanne can be assured that her one Bitcoin is secure and indisputable, forever.

**Fig. 2.3:    Value of Bitcoin Compared to Other Economic Metrics.**

![image](images/C02-FIG3.jpg)

Each and every Bitcoin transaction is readable by everyone in records stored at individual nodes. All anyone has to do is to join the network and the entire history of validated trades would be visible to them on the longest chain at the time. [Fig. 2.4](#CH002.html#fig4) illustrates Bitcoin’s approach to transaction flow and validation. In the illustration, suppose Chris receives three Bitcoins from Bob. Chris has to validate that Bob had three Bitcoins to pass on to Chris. In order to do so, Chris determines when and how did Bob receive three Bitcoins to begin with. In this example, Adam had transferred three Bitcoins to Bob, which was timestamped on the block. Next, Chris and the miners have to ascertain that Bob did not transfer those three Bitcoins previously. In other words, they have to verify absence of any transaction of spending from Bob. Once that is verified the transfer of three Bitcoins from Bob to Chris is confirmed and recorded on the block and every subsequent block.

**Fig. 2.4:    Recording of Transactions on Blocks.**

![image](images/C02-FIG4.jpg)

*Source*: Adapted from Böhme, Christin, Edelman, and Moore (2015).

Bitcoin relies on a public–private key cryptography, explained in greater technical detail in a subsequent chapter. Here we provide a simple illustration of how it works in the context of the above example, as an introduction to the underlying process. Briefly, there are two types of keys: private key and public key. Private key is known only to the user, whereas the public key is widely shared. In [Fig. 2.4](#CH002.html#fig4), Adam sends three Bitcoins to Bob by publishing the message in the Bitcoin network along with the reference to the transaction where he had obtained those three Bitcoins in the first place. The message is encrypted using Adam’s private key to indicate that the message came from him. When Bob transfers three Bitcoins to Chris, he likewise publishes a message using his private key, indicating that he is transferring to Chris the Bitcoins he had received from Adam. A Bitcoin transaction does not clear until it has been added to the consensus blockchain. In [Table 2.1](#CH002.html#tab1), it is shown that as of December 2019, a new block was being added every nine minutes. Once the transactions are recorded in a block, they are validated and are included in every subsequent block, hence form immutable permanent records.

### 2.7. BLOCKCHAIN BEYOND BITCOIN

As we have discussed throughout this chapter blockchain was the essential technology, the infrastructure, which enabled the growth of Bitcoin. Blockchain enabled Bitcoin transactions without the need for a third-party verification. Bitcoin perhaps could have functioned as a cryptocurrency, through a third-party verification scheme, much like PayPal. However, the blockchain technology enabled transactions via Bitcoin to occur without the third party, thereby increasing the popularity and functionality of Bitcoin.

**Fig. 2.5:    Bitcoin : Blockchain : Car : Road.**

![image](images/C02-FIG5.jpg)

To fully appreciate the relationship between Bitcoin and blockchain, consider the relationship between the invention of the car and the paved road. At the turn of twentieth century, when cars were first introduced, these cars had to mostly drive on cobblestone paths meant for horse-drawn carriages. While the cars could be driven on unpaved roads. (Panel 1, [Fig. 2.5](#CH002.html#fig5)), the functionality was limited. The car could not reach its full potential or speed when driving on the unpaved road, and the ride was not as comfortable. Development of asphalt roads and highways in the United States, circa 1920–1950 (Panel 2, [Fig. 2.5](#CH002.html#fig5)) tremendously aided the performance and functionality of the car. The car could drive much faster on the asphalt road than on a grassy meadow or a cobblestone pavement, and the ride was much more comfortable. However, when paved roads became common, existing or archaic modes of transportations were adapted for improved performance on the asphalt paved road. Even centuries old bullock carts could perform better on these roads when fitted with rubber tires (Panel 3, [Fig. 2.5](#CH002.html#fig5)).

Similarly, the introduction of blockchain and its incorporation into Bitcoin enabled transactions to occur between two parties without the need of a trusted third party. This reduced transaction costs, similar to reducing friction, which led to the flourishing popularity of Bitcoin akin to the performance of the car on a paved road. However, once the blockchain technology has been developed, features of it could be harnessed to improve existing business processes or develop new applications that were previously intractable. In the subsequent chapters of this book we discuss those additional applications of blockchain, beyond that of Bitcoin.
