## 3

## BASIC PRIMER ON BLOCKCHAIN TERMINOLOGY

### 3.1. INTRODUCTION

As discussed in the previous chapter, blockchain is an enabling technology, in that it facilitates immutable record-keeping. The record itself could be of any object, from sport records, educational certification, ownership or monetary transaction. The most well-known application of this new record-keeping mechanism is that in cryptocurrency, hence many people associate blockchain with Bitcoin. However, as discussed in the previous chapter, it is important to remember that Bitcoin is merely an implementation of blockchain technology.

Accessibility, anonymity, immutability and real-time sharing of information are some of the key features that make blockchain technology suitable for record-keeping in complex business arrangements. Due to these properties, blockchain enables transfer of money, property, data or any other asset type between two parties securely, anonymously and immutably. Moreover, this is achieved without having to go through expensive intermediaries, who normally would charge agent or broker fees to mediate such transactions. One of the many accepted scientific definition of blockchain is[¹](#CH003.html#fn1)

*a peer-to-peer network which timestamps records by hashing them into an ongoing chain of hash-based proof-of-work, forming a record that cannot be changed without redoing the proof-of-work.*

In this chapter, we provide a non-technical understanding of the above definition and also eradicate few popular myths about the efficacy of blockchain. For while blockchain is a valuable technology with many potential applications in business and accounting, it can’t cure all problems. It will not make traditional accounting, legal or supply chain professions obsolete. Quite to the contrary, by bringing efficiencies and reducing transactions costs, it will enable many micro-transactions which in fact would require more of the traditional services.

The original problem that blockchain solved was that of transfer of money between strangers in a secure, trusted and non-reversible fashion without going through an intermediary, such as a financial institution. This feature when extended, allows blockchain to enable money or asset transfer between unknown parties who do not necessarily trust each other. Clearly this mechanism works as the market capitalization for both Bitcoin and Ethereum are in billions of dollars, with the market capitalization of Bitcoin reaching close $300 billion and that of Ethereum to $200 billion (see [Fig. 3.1](#CH003.html#fig1)). It does so in a trusted way which is faster and costs much less than traditional alternatives. These features of the new technology give it promise and the power to disrupt traditional means of transferring assets and record-keeping.

**Fig. 3.1:    Market Capitalization of Ethereum and Bitcoin.[²](#CH003.html#fn2)**

![image](images/C03-FIG1.jpg)

In the remainder of the chapter, we will provide a non-technical description of the important technical nomenclature of blockchain and repudiate some of myths and common misrepresentations. In the next section, we discuss who the participants are and what constitutes transactions in a blockchain. In Section 3.3, we clarify how the context and definition of words in blockchain nomenclature are different than their traditional meaning or accepted meaning in accounting and business. We also address why blockchain is not “triple-entry record-keeping” or has any semblance of recording business transactions through debits and credits. In fact, entering transactions in a blockchain does not obviate the need to make traditional double-entry accounting journal entries or maintaining the accounting ledger. The technical aspects of blockchain technology, such as hashes and Merkle trees are discussed in Section 3.4. In Section 3.5, we discuss the many types of blockchain based upon membership criterion and access to data. Next, in Section 3.6, we briefly describe some of the features of blockchain which will be expanded upon in subsequent chapters. In Section 3.7, we describe a subset of the blockchain platforms currently available. Section 3.8 discusses how the popular board game Monopoly is being adapted to be played on blockchain. Section 3.9 concludes with a brief summary.

### 3.2. PARTICIPANTS AND TRANSACTIONS

The individuals or organizations engaged in a blockchain are called the participants. The participants comprise the network. Individuals or organizations decide to become part of the group that is formed with a common goal. This common goal could be any shared interest, such as transfer of money, maintaining the integrity of a supply chain for a particular industry, provenance of minerals or agricultural products. Additional examples of shared goals are presented in later chapters which discuss those applications in greater detail.

All participants of a blockchain network are equals, with no single participant having more rights or power than any other. Since the network has no single powerful participant or a “central figure” the network is not centralized, and is termed a *decentralized* network. All participants have similar access and power. Any participant in the network could communicate with any other, making use of a seamless and direct peer-to-peer communication. This peer-to-peer communication enables direct transfer of assets, currency or records between participants without having to go through a “central” figure or an intermediary. Since each participant benefits from the network they are motivated to maintain the integrity and proper functioning of the network. In a subsequent chapter, we will discuss the economic incentives of the participants to be honest and act in good faith to preserve and protect the network.

Actions taken by the participants of the network are called “transactions.” If a participant joins the network, it is a transaction. When a participant requisitions to transfer funds or assets to another participant, it is a transaction. When the other participant accepts the funds, it is a separate transaction. Interestingly, it is also a transaction when a participant leaves the network. In general, any record of the activities of the network is labeled as “transaction.”

The term “transaction” in blockchain nomenclature is over-generalized than its meaning and usage in traditional accounting and business vocabulary. In business education, we define transaction as a business event with an unrelated third party having a monetary consequence. In accounting terminology, transaction is even more precisely and narrowly defined as having an impact on the financial statements, and thereby subject to meeting certain criteria promulgated by the accounting bodies, like the FASB or IFRS.

Blockchain has the capacity to record an infinite number of transactions. The transactions take place ***only*** between the participants of the network. The blockchain will not record transactions or dealings that a member of the blockchain has with a non-member, since it does not affect the ecosystem. Each and every transaction is recorded in a data *ledger*. The term “ledger” merely denotes a book, a register or an archive of records. In the instance of blockchain, the ledger is the digital version of a traditional register.

The ledger in blockchain is digital, hence multiple identical copies of the ledger can be easily reproduced and shared with all the participants of the blockchain. This property of being able to maintain multiple identical copies of the ledger at different locations gives blockchain its key advantage over traditional record-keeping system. In traditional record-keeping with physical records, there is a unique ledger or the record book which was kept at a central and secure location. The copies of this ledger aren’t as reliable as they may not be updated in real time, hence could be incomplete. This need for a unique, secure and reliable ledger required centralization. The digitization of the ledger obviated the need of such a central figure or an intermediary.

To fully appreciate the advantage of this feature, consider the traditional system with a bank or financial institution acting as the central figure. The bank is the steward of the only ledger, which recorded all transactions. The bank is the single entity that owns, records, controls and governs the accountholders records of transactions. Thus, there is only one single copy of the individual’s record of transactions. There is only one set of books. Likewise, when we communicate with our friends and family through Facebook, all the communication goes through Facebook’s servers, hence Facebook has the ownership of all information and content passing through its servers. Blockchain technology eliminates the role of the central figure, and instead distributes that power equally to each and every participant. The data-ledger is held at each participant node, thereby removing any one party’s control over the data.

This digital ledger keeps detailed records of each and every transaction in the order they occurred. Multiple, real-time distributed copies of this ledger make it impossible for any one participant to alter the records, *ex post*. Even though any participant could alter the ledger under their control, and change a past transaction, their ledger would now be out of sync with the ledgers held by other participants, and hence invalid. This is what leads to the key advantage of blockchain – immutability of records. That is, once a transaction is recorded and accepted by a majority of participants, the record of the transaction is permanent and cannot be altered by any one participant or a small group of participants. Thus, the blockchain records and stores all transactions in the data-ledger, and all of these records are accessible to any participant.

The blockchain ledger is immutable and non-amendable. In other words, it is write-only or append-only. Participants are allowed to add new records of transactions but not allowed to modify or alter existing ones. When a transaction has to be reversed, either because it was entered in error or was not executed, a separate entry has to be made. Thus the original transaction details are preserved forever, and so is the subsequent reversal. This form of appending ensures that the record shows that the transaction occurred initially, and was reversed on a later date. Whereas allowing modification and alteration would lead to deletion of the original transaction. It is important to understand the nuance, in a blockchain all participants even at later date would be able to distinguish between events which led to a transaction that was subsequently reversed and those non-events that never led to any transactions being recorded.

To summarize, the key terminology and nomenclature in blockchain may have slightly different meaning or nuance than in its common usage. A ***participant*** or a ***member*** is any individual or organization that shares the same goal or interest with other participants and they collectively form a ***network***. The network is formed and maintained by the members for a certain purpose, the rules and protocol in the network are governed by the members who have a shared goal or interest. Every action of the members, such as joining the network, requests to other members, accepting of another member’s request, exchange of goods or money and leaving the network, are called ***transactions***. Each and every transaction is recorded in a data ***ledger***. The data-ledger is shared real time with all participants, hence multiple identical copies of the data-ledger is preserved. Every new transaction has to be accepted, then recorded in the ledger and the ledger shared with all participants. The difference in meaning and their implications will be discussed in the next section.

### 3.3. ONE WORD, TWO MEANINGS AND OTHER MISCONCEPTIONS

As noted earlier, “transaction” in blockchain lingo is not the same as what we define as transaction in normal accounting and business usage. From a blockchain perspective, every interaction a participant has within the blockchain ecosystem is defined as transactions, only a fraction of those would qualify as a transaction in an accounting context. Knowing the difference between the two will be a useful skill as business processes and dealings increasingly transition to a blockchain domain. While some insinuate that blockchain will replace the accounting profession, it hardly seems so. Quite to the contrary, blockchain might actually make certain accounting skills and knowledge more relevant.[³](#CH003.html#fn3) While, blockchain surely would have effect on some accounting procedures and processes, it will also create the need for new skill-sets to be developed within the context of accounting and business. For example, the skill and knowledge to identify “transactions” from blockchain which ought to be recorded as an accounting transaction will continue to be relevant. This culling of “transactions” has to be both valid and comprehensive, in that all and only those transactions that have a bearing in an accounting context have to be selected, and none others. The skill and ability to do so will require a grasp of pertinent and current accounting standards. For example, ordering of goods from a supplier in a blockchain context is a “transaction,” but it is not recorded as such in accounting.

The data-ledger is a digital record of all transactions in a blockchain. As previously discussed, this ledger is shared between each participant of the networks. Each participant maintains an up to date copy of the ledger. This feature makes it impossible for any one member to alter the records in the ledger stored with them, since they will be unable to alter the records in ledgers held by other participants. Without getting the approval of the other members, the alterations made will not be recognized as being valid. This makes it impossible for any one member to alter their version of data-records, leading to the immutability feature of the blockchain.

In contrast, ledger in an accounting context is a book containing all the accounts in which information from accounting “journal entries” are posted as debits and credits. Again, while using the identical word, the meaning is very different. There is no notion of “debits” or “credits” in the data-ledger, it is merely a compilation of all transaction regardless of whether or not it has an impact on the financial statement. Whereas a ledger in accounting context maintains a running total of account balances. Each accounting transaction is recorded in at least two ledger accounts, using the terminology of “debits” and “credits.” This process will continue to persist even when blockchain is used. In that sense, the data-ledger of a blockchain is not a replacement or a substitute for the accounting ledger. They do not undertake the same function, hence are likely to coexist.

In blockchain terminology, an asset is anything of value that members are willing to exchange in the network. It could be currency, property, cryptocurrency, pictures, records, company shares, etc. This is not consistent with the accounting definition of asset. An asset as defined in accounting has to meet certain criteria defined by FASB or IFRS. Assets, in an accounting context, are resources owned by a business which has the potential of generating future cash flow for the company, and its value can be objectively monetized. For example, a patent or copyright of intellectual property can be termed as an asset in a blockchain, but not for accounting purposes. One such blockchain application is described in a later chapter, wherein the musicians create, share and transact in original music through blockchain. The content that is traded is termed an “asset” in blockchain terminology but will not be termed as an asset for accounting purposes as it does not meet the necessary criteria required for recognition as an asset, that is, objective valuation.

A very common misconception, promulgated mostly by non-accounting computer scientist, is that blockchain is a triple-entry bookkeeping system.[⁴](#CH003.html#fn4) The term does have a *zing,* and of course “triple” is better and more secure than “double,” it easily conveys that blockchain technology is an improvement over current accounting processes. While that may arguably be so, the use of term “triple-entry” to describe the accounting implication for blockchain is incorrect. It is naively assumed that accounting transactions between two parties are mirror images of each other, that is, if one company has a revenue other must have an expense; and if one party records accounts receivable the other party must record an account payable.

It may come as a shock to non-accountants, but combined balance sheets, all over the world, do not have to balance. Mirror accounting transaction between two parties is a simplified notion of accounting, and definitely not the case with accrual accounting. Take a simple transaction, that of purchase of inventory. While the supplier selling the inventory records a revenue, the buyer records the purchase as inventory, as an asset, and not as an expense. For the buyer the inventory becomes an expense, cost of goods sold, only when it eventually sells those items or goods produced from those items to another customer. The situation gets even more complicated in transactions involving capital lease payments and those involving foreign currency transactions. Even for straightforward transaction of a receivable for one company leading to payable for another, the symmetry is not guaranteed. Accounting rules requires the recording of receivables to undergo an evaluation that the amount is expected to be collected. When in doubt, no receivable is recorded, while the owing company will still have to record the payable.

Despite publications to the contrary, in popular business press, blockchain is not equivalent to triple bottom line accounting,[⁵](#CH003.html#fn5) these nuances of accounting are not fully understood by computer scientist leading to exaggerated claims on the efficacy of blockchain and its ability to replace traditional accounting functions. Moreover, even the term “triple-entry bookkeeping” was coined by a prominent accounting scholar, Yuri Ijiri (1986), a professor at the Carnegie Mellon University. His notion was to quantify the concept that changes in wealth must be equal to income, and added concepts to make that determination palpable. Hence, the use of the term “triple-entry bookkeeping” to describe the functions of blockchain is erroneous.

### 3.4. BLOCKS, HASHES AND MERKLE TREES

Transactions are detailed records of actions of members and interactions between them that are recorded in a ***block***. Each participant can access, verify and accept the transactions noted in a block. Once a majority of participants accept the block, it is chained to the block that is the terminal block of the longest chain at the time. The identification of the legitimate block where the new block will be attached is accomplished through a ***hash function***.

Blocks maintain the indestructible record of transactions. Hence, whenever there are new transactions, a new block is created. Once created, the block has to be approved by the participants of the network using a pre-determined rule (discussed later) and then appended to the existing chain. After the new block has been appended to the chain, it cannot be removed or amended. These blocks are linked to each other, forming a blockchain. Each block includes a cryptographic hash of the prior block of the chain, which itself is chained through a “hash” with the preceding block, and so on. The first block in the chain is called the ***genesis block***, and subsequent blocks are sequentially chained to the genesis block.

**Fig. 3.2:    Illustration of Blocks and Hashes.**

![image](images/C03-FIG2.jpg)

The time it takes to create and append a new block is called the ***block time***. The block time varies across different blockchain networks. Shorter the block time, the quicker new transactions get recorded and appended to the chain. In 2019, the block time for Ethereum platform was between 12 and 15 seconds, with the highest being about 30 seconds. In contrast, the recent (2018 onwards) block time for Bitcoin has ranged between 10 to 12 minutes.[⁶](#CH003.html#fn6)

The formation of blocks and their addition to the existing chain is illustrated in [Fig. 3.2](#CH003.html#fig2). The first block at the bottom is the genesis block. The next block is added to this block. At some of the points there is a ***fork***, where two blocks were processed, however, the one to which the next block gets added first becomes the predominant chain and continues to grow, whereas the offshoot branch does not grow. This is established by the underlying feature of blockchain that the longest chain is the valid chain, hence new blocks are added to the longest chain at the time, thereby stubbing or stumping the offshoots.

The hash function is a mapping algorithm as shows in [Fig. 3.2](#CH003.html#fig2) in that it changes one sequence to another sequence. In other words, it generates a value from a string of text using a mathematical function. Hashing takes on an input of any length and converts it into an output of a fixed length.[⁷](#CH003.html#fn7) Each transformation is unique and reversible. That is, upon knowing the hash function one could reverse engineer to decode the original message. The process of hashing helps make the transmission of message tamper-free.

The hash function ensures that every time the algorithm is executed with the same electronic record as its input it gives the same output or result. This makes it computationally infeasible to derive or reconstruct the original electronic record without the hash result produced by the algorithm. Since each hash function is unique it is impossible that two electronic records using the algorithm can produce the same hash result. This is also known as the deterministic property of the hash function. The hash function is also capable of quick computation to ensure efficiency of the system.

A key property of the hash function is that since it is cryptographically generated, even small changes to the input could result in large differences in the resultant hash function. The immutability quality of blockchain is derived from this property of the hash function, ensuring that any minor changes to the recorded data could result in large changes in the hash function. Another key property of hash is that each and every string of data should have a unique resulting hash, as shown in [Fig. 3.2](#CH003.html#fig2). This is an important property of the hashing function to ensure that the original message would be uniquely retrieved once we have the hash, and it is remote that two or more original messages will be retrieved for a single hash.

The blockchain is a linked list that contains data and hash pointer that points each block to its preceding block as shown in [Fig. 3.3](#CH003.html#fig3). A hash pointer not only contains the address of the previous block but also the hash of the data inside the previous block. This minor feature of the hash pointer led to the key immutability property of blockchain. Maintaining the hash of the data inside the previous block ensures that if the data residing in the previous block is altered, even marginally, it will lead to a different hash function. The resulting hash function will not correspond to that stored in the subsequent block. Hence, for the tampering of data in previous blocks to be successful, it would require mining of every subsequent block that now incorporates the different hash.

**Fig. 3.3:    Formation of Blockchain.**

![image](images/C03-FIG3.jpg)

The concept of Merkle tree was developed by Ralph Merkle and patented in 1979.[⁸](#CH003.html#fn8) It is fundamentally a data structure tree, an unique organization of the storage of data that makes it easier to retrieve any specific records. In blockchain, each block contains thousands of transactions, hence without a proper retrieval mechanism locating a particular transaction would be extremely time-consuming and cumbersome. Organization of data in a Merkle tree makes it extremely efficient to retrieve the particulars of any transaction stored in a block, hence making it easier to verify the validity and occurrence of each and every historical transaction.

An important feature of Merkle tree structure is its ability to authenticate large sets of data. It uses a “divide and conquer” strategy, by distributing large sets of data into manageable smaller parts which individually are easier to verify. Following the terminology of tree, Merkle tree comprises of a root, branches and many leaves. The root contains the fingerprint of the entire data set. Bitcoin, for example, uses an SHA-256 algorithm. SHA stands for “Secure Hashing Algorithm,” and 256 denotes the fixed size of the output.

Merkle trees and hashing functions form the computing and mathematical backbone of blockchain. The distinctive features of Merkle trees and the hashing function combined gives the blockchain its valued properties. Merkle tree structure makes it feasible to store large amounts of data and easily retrieve information upon request. Additionally, the hash function provides the feature which makes it difficult to alter records or data encrypted in past blocks, giving blockchains the property of immutability.

### 3.5. TYPES OF BLOCKCHAIN

There are some variations in who can participate in the blockchain and how accessible are the records stored in the blockchain. These variations lead to two sets of blockchain: permissioned or non-permissioned; and public or private.

The distinction between permissioned and non-permissioned network is based upon who can join the network. Any individual or organization can at any time join a non-permissioned network. The entry to and the exit from the network are not monitored, hence participants have the flexibility of joining and leaving at will. In contrast, a permissioned network does not allow new participants to join without obtaining permission from the owners or creators of the network.

In a public network, transactions between members are visible to all participants in the network. In contrast, private networks allow only the parties to the transaction to be able to access and view the transaction. Thus, private networks ensure confidentiality of transactions by restricting public access to those transactions.

These two variations lead to four possible permutations of blockchain, as follows:

**1.** A public and non-permissioned network is the most open and transparent form. Any individual or organization can join and leave the network at will. Once the individual becomes a participant in the network, they will gain access to all present and historical transactions conducted in the network.

**2.** Private and non-permissioned networks allow for wide participation, enabling individuals and organization to join at will. However, the details of transaction are available only to the members who were parties to the transaction. Consequently, new participants cannot view historical transactions, as they could not have been party to those.

**3.** Public and permissioned networks restrict which individual or organization can join the network. Usually such blockchains are restricted only to the founding participants, and subsequent participants are invited to join. Most corporate blockchain, or those between organizations in a supply chain are permissioned networks. Once an individual or organization is allowed to join the blockchain they have access to the details of all transactions recorded in the network.

**4.** A private and permissioned network is the most restrictive form of blockchain. Not only does the blockchain restricts membership in the network but also restricts information to the participants in the network. Each participant is privy to the details of only those transactions that they engaged in, and not that of others.

The blockchain network property is important to regulators, as certain configurations makes it easier for regulators to monitor and control the network. From a regulatory perspective, public and non-permissioned network is the easiest to monitor due to unrestricted access to all transactions.

Bitcoin is a public and non-permissioned network; hence anyone can join the network at any time and have access to all the historical records. The Internal Revenue Service (IRS), the taxing authority in the United States, took advantage of this feature in auditing and taxing individuals who were mining and transacting in Bitcoins. IRS is becoming increasingly adept at tracing digital currency around the globe with support from international agencies and coin exchanges.[⁹](#CH003.html#fn9) IRS directly consults with blockchain companies like Chainalysis which allows IRS to be one step ahead of the participants and helps them identify money launderers, tax evaders and those engaging in nefarious transactions.

A summary of the comparison of features of permissioned and non-permissioned blockchain is presented in [Fig. 3.4](#CH003.html#fig4).

**Fig. 3.4:    A Comparison of Features between Permissioned and Non-permissioned Networks.**

![image](images/C03-FIG4.jpg)

### 3.6. ESSENTIAL FEATURES OF BLOCKCHAIN

There are some essential features of blockchain that will be presented in greater details in subsequent chapters. This section provides a brief introduction to those main features of blockchain: the consensus mechanism, smart contracts and tokenization.

The consensus mechanism is akin to the constitution or overarching rule that governs the blockchain network. It is the rules and prescribed methods by which the participants of the network can reach a collective agreement. The mechanism enables multiple participants to agree upon one version of *truth*. As the state or the truth changes with each transaction, the consensus mechanism should enable the participants to agree upon the truth continuously as new transactions occur and are appended to the blocks. This mechanism enables how and when the network decides to append new transactions into the data-ledger. There are different types of consensus mechanisms which are discussed in greater detail in the next chapter.

In most applications of blockchains there are fees to execute transactions. The fee is paid by the transacting parties and is collected by the participant or the node that appends the transaction into a block. This is also called the ***gas*** and it is a measure of computational power needed to execute the transaction, and the ***gas cost*** is the amount of fees paid.

Smart contracts are another key feature of blockchains and are simple business rules that are coded into the network. A transaction can occur only when the prescribed business logic is satisfied. For example, prior to transferring funds from the buyer to the seller, the network verifies that the buyer has sufficient fund balance. Once these types of business rules are coded into the network, these are auto-executed. When the prescribed conditions are not satisfied the transactions are blocked. Smart contracts are discussed in detail in a subsequent chapter.

Another common misnomer is with the overuse of the term “smart contract.” While the term “contract” is used, these contracts do not have any legal standing, instead these are merely simple business rules. Smart contracts are self-executory, in the sense that when the pre-conditions are satisfied the contract automatically transfers currency or assets from one participant to another. These contracts follow simple, “if-then-else” rules, enabling faster and error-free transactions, but have no legal bearing.

Tokenization is another key feature of blockchains. Simply put, it is digitization of any asset that can be exchanged through a blockchain. The tokenization makes the asset tradable. The underlying asset could be property, expensive painting or even a piece of music. This concept is discussed in greater detail in a subsequent chapter.

### 3.7. BLOCKCHAIN PLATFORMS

There are many blockchain platforms that have been implemented for different purposes. Blockchain platforms provide an ecosystem of different technologies that solves a specific problem. Bitcoin is one such platform and was implemented to facilitate exchange of currency between participants. Many more platforms have emerged since Bitcoin. Some of the common platforms are shown in [Fig. 3.5](#CH003.html#fig5) and a brief description of some are discussed in this section.

**Fig. 3.5:    Blockchain Platforms.**

![image](images/C03-FIG5.jpg)

At the age of 19, a Russian-Canadian teenager Vitalik Buterin published the white paper, which went on to create Ethereum in 2015 as an open-source blockchain platform. It was the second public network and is an open and adaptable platform that allows anyone to build decentralized applications and network. The concept of smart contracts, though around for two decades, was popularized through the Ethereum platform and has become a building block for blockchain concept. Since its introduction, Ethereum has become the largest blockchain network with over 300,000 developers and over 2,000 decentralized applications (dApp).[^(10)](#CH003.html#fn10) There are many new solutions, networks and currencies that are built on the Ethereum platform.

Hyperledger is a collaborative effort between different companies in various industries including, banking, e-commerce, Internet of Things (IoT), supply chains, manufacturing and technology. One of the successful projects is the Hyperledger-Fabric developed by IBM enabling business to business transactions. Hyperledger-Fabric provides the infrastructure that allows businesses to quickly and easily create their own private networks and data-ledger.

Other common platforms are shown in [Fig. 3.5](#CH003.html#fig5), and a brief description and their websites for a subset of those are as follows:

*   NEM (nem.io) is a platform to develop permissioned, private blockchain and uses a novel consensus mechanism: proof of importance.
    
*   EOS (eos.io) was launched in June 2018 as an open-source software and supports both private and public blockchain.
    
*   Corda ([corda.net](http://corda.net)) is another open-source blockchain provider launched in November 2016 and was developed by R3 in collaboration with over 200 technology and industry partners. It enables private transactions and immediate finality.
    
*   IPFS (ipfs.io) is a peer-to-peer hypermedia protocol designed to make the web faster, safer and more open through incorporation of blockchain technology.
    
*   Quorum ([goquororum.com](http://goquororum.com)) is an open-source blockchain platform that is based on Ehtereum. It provides full-service support to its customers.
    
*   IOTA ([www.iota.org](http://www.iota.org)) is an open-source distributed ledger designed to power the IoT with feeless transactions and a scalable network.
    
*   Open Chain ([openchain.org](http://openchain.org)) is a distributed ledger system that caters to organizations wishing to manage digital assets. It uses partitioned consensus to validate transactions in its platform.
    
*   NEO ([neo.org](http://neo.org)), according to their website, is a “non-profit community-based blockchain project that … digitize assets, to automate the management of digital assets using smart contracts, and to realize a ‘smart economy’ with a distributed network.”
    
*   Mongo DB ([mongodb.com](http://mongodb.com)) is a document-based distributed database built for the cloud era.
    

### 3.8. MONOPOLY ON BLOCKCHAIN

To better understand the application and power of blockchain technology, consider the following thought experiment. Imagine playing the popular Monopoly game, but without cash transactions. Imagine the game is played as usual, but each and every player keeps detailed records of their transactions throughout the game, only to be settled at the end of the game. Each player maintains their personal ledger, and only has access to their ledger and not that of any other player until the end of the game. From an accounting perspective, each player prepares journal entries, books those entries into a ledger, through the traditional method of debits and credits, and prepares an income statement and a balance sheet. Suppose six accountants are playing the game, what are the chances that all the six balance sheets will balance and that the “Universal” balance sheet of the game will balance as well. There are bound to be disputes and arguments at the end of the game on one or more previous transactions.

In a blockchain enabled Monopoly game the recording of data is digital, and the data-ledger is shared with all players. The first block, or the genesis block, records the start of the game, that of each player joining, receiving cash and some properties at the start of the game. As the game proceeds, each roll of the dice and each move of each player is recorded as data. Once other players agree to the transaction, based on a built-in consensus mechanism, the data gets transcribed to a block. After a series of transactions, say each round, the block gets appended to the previous block, forming the blockchain. Thus, in this simplification, rather than maintaining six separate ledgers only one data-ledger needs to be maintained. Each player is still expected to keep track of their own transactions and aggregate those. However, the unified data-ledger ensures that no player can change any of the previous agreed outcomes, and thereby cannot cheat. Moreover, disputes caused by manual errors or difference in interpretation could easily be resolved by referring back to the transaction recorded on the immutable blockchain.

Recently, some investors invested a combined $2 million in Uplandme to develop the digital Monopoly on the EOS blockchain.[^(11)](#CH003.html#fn11) Based on the key aspects of Monopoly, the game will enable players to buy, sell, trade imaginary properties based on real world address. However, instead of paper cards, the game uses non-fungible tokens to confer ownership of an asset. The game is currently in the Beta version, where players are able to trade properties in virtual San Francisco.

Hasbro, the corporate owners of Monopoly, are not far behind. It too is committed to build a decentralized and community autonomous game platform.[^(12)](#CH003.html#fn12) It is attempting to solve some existing problems with online games through its integration of blockchain and through forming a community ecosystem. They too are looking at developing “Monopoly Classic” that will be available for playing online. They are incorporating many aspects of blockchain technology, such as the consensus mechanism and digitally shared ledger.

### 3.9. SUMMARY

**Fig. 3.6:    Key Terms and Definitions.**

![image](images/C03-FIG6.jpg)

In this chapter, we presented non-technical definitions of basic blockchain terminology. Some commonly used words have been adopted in blockchain nomenclature with vastly different meaning. These differences in nuances are important in fully comprehending the scope and functionality of blockchain. Additionally some terms, with established meaning and usage, have been misused in the context of blockchain. We attempted to clarify those confusions, prior to discussing the blockchain concept in greater technical detail.

This chapter also discussed the various types of blockchain, their properties and advantages. It also presented a brief description of many current blockchain platforms. The adaptation of the popular board game Monopoly to harness the capability of blockchain technology has the potential of increasing the general public’s comfort level with this new technology. Finally, [Fig. 3.6](#CH003.html#fig6) provides a pictorial summary of the key concepts of blockchain and their definitions.

## 4

## THE CONSENSUS MECHANISM

### 4.1. INTRODUCTION

Blockchain is a system that enables unknown and untrusting parties to transact with each other, and the records of these transactions are maintained in blocks. The blocks are connected sequentially, so that the ordering and precedence of transactions are preserved forever. The mechanism itself is self-executory in that there is no central administration that is making the rules or ad hoc determinations, rather it is the collective decision of *all* the participants in the network. Each participant has an equal vote. Since it is feasible that different participants can reach different conclusions based on the order in which the messages were received, the system ought to be able to quickly resolve those disagreements and ensure that there is a majority agreement.

The participants in the network all have equal rights, hence can be considered *peers*. However, the peers can fail arbitrarily, and some peers may be malicious. For the system to be effective it should have a built-in mechanism to ensure the validity of the output, even when some peers are malicious or are malfunctioning.

The network itself requires minimal structure. Participants can leave or rejoin the network as and when they please. Yet the system is able to attain consensus despite arbitrary failure of some nodes, or even in presence of malicious behavior on part of some nodes. This is a critical feature of blockchain technology and is known as the consensus mechanism. As long as a majority of CPU power is controlled by nodes that are not malicious, these properly functioning nodes will collectively generate the longest chain and outpace attackers or malicious nodes. Additional participants joining the network, or even those who had left and are re-joining the network, will simply accept the longest chain, at the time, as the legitimate one and a record of what occurred while they were away from the network.

There are two popular and proven techniques that have been adopted in blockchains: the proof of work and the proof of stake. The “proof or work” technique requires a proof of computation and solving of a complex crypto-puzzle. The difficulty of the computational work limits the rate at which new blocks can be added, and also the participant who could add the block, thereby making it difficult for malicious or non-functional nodes from adding new blocks with incorrect information. The “proof of stake” grants the participant with a higher stake in the network a higher chance of adding the block, thereby again preventing minor and malicious nodes from sabotaging the network.

Nakamoto proposed a consensus mechanism in his treatise. This system does not guarantee that there will always be an agreement or consensus, but guarantees that the probability of reaching a consensus will be close to one. The probability approaches one, as more and more non-malicious participants enter the network. Increasing the difficulty of mining a new block leads to requiring more time to insert the new block, and makes it rare that multiple solutions would gain traction. Ethereum also builds upon the proof of work mechanism to validate new blocks. However, Ethereum system generates a new block in 12–15 seconds relative to Bitcoin requiring about 10 minutes to mine a new block. Consequently, the Ethereum system improves throughput to recording transactions, it also leads to multiple solutions, as different miners will be able to simultaneously propose alternative blocks. To avoid this occurrence the protocol has instituted safeguards as discussed in Wood (2014).

The remainder of the chapter is organized as follows. In Section 4.2, we discuss the importance of achieving consensus. In Section 4.3, we present the abstract computer science problem, popularly known as the Byzantine Generals Problem. In Section 4.4, we discuss the economic incentives of the participants. In Section 4.5, we discuss the widespread blockchain consensus mechanism, the proof of work. Another consensus mechanism, the proof of stake, is described in Section 4.6. Some threats to these consensus mechanisms are discussed in Section 4.7. We conclude with a summary in Section 4.8.

### 4.2. IMPORTANCE OF CONSENSUS

The consensus mechanism is essential to ensure that there is only one correct order of the blocks. The blockchain grows through the addition of new blocks to the existing chain. Hence it is imperative that not just the new block be verified but also the current chain and the ordering of the blocks in that chain are verified. This process is executed such that new blocks are appended to the chain, and multiple sequencing of these new blocks are possible. The possibility of multiple sequencing results in a branching process, commonly called a *fork*. Once a fork is discovered, it has to be resolved quickly, to ensure that the *chain* does not transform into a *tree*. [Fig. 4.1](#CH004.html#fig1) graphically illustrates the concept. The current blocks *A* through *E* constitute the current chain, and suppose two more blocks have to be added *F* and *G*. Now consider that some of the chains append *F* before *G,* and others append *G* before *F*, this results in a fork. When additional blocks *H, I* and *J* are added, the fork transforms the chain into a tree. Consequently, a mechanism is needed to quickly resolve the forks and prevent the chain from becoming a tree.

**Fig. 4.1:    Adding New Blocks to existing Chain.**

![image](images/C04-FIG1.jpg)

Extending the example above, it is conceivable that there could be a variation in the ordering of how the new blocks in Phase 2, *H, I* and *J* are ordered. If those variations are allowed, the tree forms more branches and spreads. The consensus algorithm resolves the forks quickly, thereby preventing the branches from being formed, and ensuring that the chain does not transform to a tree.

It is natural in any system that the forks do get formed as different participants of the blockchain may not receive the new information in the same sequence. However, once the fork is discovered, in other words when the participants learn about branches, there has to be a mechanism to agree on which branch is valid and prune out the rest. This ensures that only the main trunk will be allowed to propagate, preventing a tree from forming. Typically, the longest branch is adopted as the valid one in both Bitcoin and Ethereum platforms, though there is a slight difference on how “longest” is defined in these platforms (Natoli & Gramoli, 2016). The forks are not necessarily resolved right away but can grow to some limited depth. Extending a branch requires solving of puzzles that result in additional computational exercise over a longer time period, during which the *longest chain* is identified and propagated. Bitcoin recommends that six blocks be mined after a transaction is issued to consider the transaction accepted by the system, similarly Ethereum recommends that five to 11 additional blocks be appended (Wood, 2014).

Consensus has three main attributes: agreement, validity and termination. Agreement indicates that two or more parties independently reach identical conclusion and both are aware that they have reached the same conclusion. In other words, merely arriving at the same conclusion isn’t sufficient, but each party has to be aware and confident that others must have reached the same conclusion. Hence, the procedure or algorithm of reaching conclusions should be objective, unambiguous and universal. Validity indicates that the final solution is decided from one of the proposed ones. The network or system should take into account the provision that there may be some valid participants and some malicious ones. Hence, termination indicates that the final solution is the one decided by a valid participant and not by the malicious participant (Natoli & Gramoli 2016).

### 4.3. BYZANTINE GENERALS PROBLEM

In the early days of computer networks, a perennial problem that arose was the ability of a computer system to continue operating reliably, despite failure of one or more of its components. The information sent by the failed component is ignored or overlooked, as the messages from the failed component could be conflicting to those send by other parts of the system. This abstract problem was referred to as the Byzantine Generals Problem (Shostak, Pease, & Lamport, 1982).

The Byzantine Generals Problem is a hypothetical problem of attacking army regiments surrounding the enemy fort. Each regiment has its own general, and the generals can communicate with each other only through messengers. The generals must decide upon a common plan of action, to assure coordinated attack. To introduce points of failure in the system, some of the generals are assumed to be traitors. The traitors cannot change the course of action, but they can prevent the loyal generals from reaching a consensus.

To simplify the problem further, it is assumed that the regiments have only two courses of action: attack or retreat. All generals use the same method, or algorithm, to aggregate information received from different sources, to reach individual decisions. The success of attack depends on whether all regiments attack simultaneously. If only some regiments attack, while others retreat, the attacking regiments will be annihilated. That is reaching a consensus is imperative. Not only should each general reach the same conclusion, but they should have the confidence that other generals would have done so, as well. [Fig. 4.2](#CH004.html#fig2) illustrates the problem through a picture.

**Fig. 4.2:    Byzantine Generals Problem – Attack or Retreat.**

![image](images/C04-FIG2.jpg)

The difficulty of the Byzantine Generals Problem is that no solution is possible unless more than two-thirds of the generals are loyal. In particular, as shown in [Fig. 4.3](#CH004.html#fig3), with only three generals no consensus can be reached in presence of a single traitor. As the message is controlled solely by the sender, the traitor can send any possible message. In a computer system, this artifact corresponds to the messages sent by computers to each other (Shostak et al., 1982). Consider [Fig. 4.3](#CH004.html#fig3), in which the commander is loyal and sends identical message to both generals asking them to attack. However, one of the generals is a traitor and therefore sends the opposite message to the other general and asks him to retreat. The loyal general receives conflicting messages, hence cannot decide on a plan of action.

**Fig. 4.3:    Simplified Byzantine Generals Problem with One General being Traitor.**

![image](images/C04-FIG3.jpg)

**Fig. 4.4:    Byzantine Generals Problem When Commander is Traitor.**

![image](images/C04-FIG4.jpg)

Now consider a slightly different scenario, as shown in [Fig. 4.4](#CH004.html#fig4), in which the commander is a traitor and sends conflicting messages to the two generals. To one general he asks to attack and to the other he asks to retreat. The generals, being loyal, transmit the message each received from the commander. As one would note for the loyal general the messages in both Figs. [4.3](#CH004.html#fig3) and [4.4](#CH004.html#fig4) are identical, and there is no way for this general to distinguish between these two situations. So if the loyal general always follows the message he receives from the commander, he must attack in both situations, while the other general will retreat in both situations. Since one general attacks, while the other retreats, the attack will fail in both situations.

It has been shown, through proof by contradiction, that no solution is possible in a system with fewer than 3*n*+1 generals in the presence of *n* traitors. In [Fig. 4.5](#CH004.html#fig5), we illustrate how four generals can cope with one traitor in their midst. First, let’s assume that the commander is loyal but one of the generals is a traitor and changes the message. However, as long as the loyal generals adhere to the majority message, they will take the unified action.

**Fig. 4.5:    Extended Byzantine Generals Problem with One Traitor.**

![image](images/C04-FIG5.jpg)

Suppose General 1 is the traitor and alters the message from the commander, however General 2 is loyal and transmits the same message as he received from the commander. If commander transmitted identical messages to all the generals, General 3 will get two similar messages and one contradictory. Similarly, of the three messages received by General 2, two are for attack (from commander and General 3) and one is for retreat (from General 1). All three messages received by General 1 are consistent to attack. In this scenario, the consensus will be to attack, and all three generals will comply. Note that the traitor can only send the wrong message to hinder reaching the consensus, however must follow the consensus once it has been decided.

The situation in [Fig. 4.5](#CH004.html#fig5) could be altered to make the commander the traitor who sends out inconsistent messages to the three generals. Suppose the commander is restricted to choose between two alternative messages: attack or retreat. The commander instructs General 1 and General 3 to attack and instructs General 2 to retreat. Each general being loyal, dutifully transmits the message he received. Thus Generals 1 and 3 will instruct the other two generals to attack, and General 2 will instruct the other two generals to retreat. [Fig. 4.6](#CH004.html#fig6) illustrates the messages received by each of the generals. As shown, all three generals receive two messages to attack and one to retreat. Again, if they are following the majority rule, all three generals will attack, foiling the plans of the treasonous commander.

**Fig. 4.6:    Byzantine Generals Problem with Three Loyal Generals and Traitor Commander.**

![image](images/C04-FIG6.jpg)

The situation gets more complex once the message doesn’t have to be binary but could have multiple values. The example above could be extended by including a third message of “unsure,” that is the commander or generals could send one of three messages: attack, retreat and unsure. In that instance, the complexity of finding a consensus between all the general and the commander is increased. Now imagine the exponential increase in complexity when there are hundreds, or even thousands, of generals.

Additionally, the message could be a continuous variable. In our example, rather than deciding whether to attack or retreat, suppose the commander sends out the time of attack. All generals have to attack within 10 minutes of each other for the attack to be successful. A traitor would change the time of attack randomly and send wrong messages to other generals. For the system to work, the loyal generals should be able to independently arrive at the same conclusion on the time of attack, in essence overriding or ignoring the message received from the traitors.

This Byzantine Generals Problem is applicable to every distributed network, as some nodes in the network could be malicious or simply fail to send message. The other functioning nodes should be able to function and reach consensus even though not all the nodes are properly functioning. The solution to this abstract problem requires an algorithm that each node must follow. Additionally, the vulnerability of the system to malicious and non-functional nodes must be pre-determined. Mathematically, it has been shown that the system would function if the traitor nodes are less than one-third of total nodes, that is for a system with *n* malicious nodes, the total number of nodes must be 3*n* +1 for the system to reach consensus.

In a blockchain network, the above problem is even more complex as there is no true commander in the system. All participating nodes are the Generals and have the same hierarchical position. All participating nodes have to agree upon every message that is transmitted between the nodes, even though there is no node that is superior to others. The system should be able to function even when a node or a group of nodes are corrupt or malicious, in that the message sent from these nodes are unreliable. In summary, the participants in the blockchain should be able to arrive at a consensus even when some of the participants are malicious or dysfunctional and are sending inaccurate messages.

### 4.4. ECONOMICS OF VALIDITY AND VERIFIABILITY

The validity and propagation of blockchain requires effort of many unrelated participants. These participants have to be compensated in some form to ensure their continued engagement in the chain. These incentives are usually funded through transaction fees. The transacting parties that want their transaction to be facilitated through blockchain would pay a nominal transaction fee. The transaction fee is then collected by a “miner” who mines the next block, either by solving a complex cryptographic puzzle or by winning a random drawing, depending on the consensus mechanism.

Another way that miners are compensated is through inflation. As the currency they had initially mined become more valuable, their wealth grows, without having to do any additional work. Hence, initial miners being motivated to preserve the value of their coins and even grow those, would encourage growth in the system and additional transactions being recorded. Moreover, they are vested in the security and credibility of the system and wouldn’t want those to be compromised.

The incentive in blockchain, through transaction fee and inflation, encourage participating nodes to stay honest. Even if a malicious attacker is able to assemble sufficient CPU power, to out-compute honest nodes, they should have greater incentive to block new blocks rather than alter the existing ones. In other words, the malicious node with sufficient CPU power should have the opportunity of generating greater profits by mining additional coins than by going back and reversing transactions. That is the incentives of playing by the rules should exceed that of defrauding the system. The cost of undermining the system would further reduce the value of the coins or wealth the malicious node has amassed.

To safeguard the sanctity of the system, the cost to defraud should be made large relative to the ill-gotten gains. Thus, the benefit for a miscreant stealing from the system would be less than the cost incurred thereby making it monetarily unattractive for the miscreant to steal. In the Bitcoin setting this is accomplished naturally through the setup. First the miners have to purchase expensive hardware equipment that can solve the mining algorithm. The miscreant would have to invest in computation capacity and thus have a high investment in the system. Moreover, if the mining process is subverted the reputation and credibility of Bitcoin is adversely affected, reducing its value. Thus the coins that the miscreant stole from the system would be worth less, and would become lower as the miscreant steals more and consequently damages the credibility of the process. Structuring the process as such, whereby the value of the currency is based on its reputation, deters miscreants to steal thereby damaging the reputation, and consequently diminishing the value of the very object they are stealing.

### 4.5. PROOF OF WORK

Nakamoto faced the challenge of “establishing trust in a trustless network.” He had to rely on a system that would ensure that it will be impossible for an individual or even a small group, to computationally outpace the collective network. Thus the basic idea was to make it difficult to add blocks to the blockchain, and would require substantial computational power. This is the common feature of “proof of work” algorithm.

Proof of work is one of the consensus mechanisms for achieving agreement on the blockchain network to confirm transactions and produce new blocks to the chain. The proof of work solves the Byzantine Generals Problem of determining representation in majority decision-making. Rather than assigning votes based on participation, it assigns votes based on processing ability. Proof of work is essentially a vote for each unit of CPU. This mechanism of voting, rather than one vote per participant prevents subversion by an attacker who is able to obtain many marginal participants.

Of the many consensus protocols, the proof of work consensus mechanism accounts for a considerable share (around 90%) of the total market capitalization of existing digital currencies.[¹](#CH004.html#fn1) Introduced through Bitcoin, the proof of work consensus mechanism has been adopted by Litecoin, Dogecoin and Ethereum. It has been widely adopted even in spheres outside of digital currency.

It is important to prevent malicious participants from corrupting the network. The proof of work mechanism reduces the relative gains from fraudulent behavior by adding costs to engage in those. The costs in the case of Bitcoin is the computational costs, the hardware and the electricity consumption (also known as “gas”). The validators establish their authenticity by revealing to the network answers to computationally difficult cryptographic puzzles.

In the proof of work mechanism, the miners compete against each other to validate the next set of transactions. The successful miner gets rewarded through transaction fees to build the next block. The probability of being selected to build the next block, and thus get rewarded, is proportional to the computation power. As will be discussed later, the miners compete against each other to solve a complex cryptographic puzzle. Having greater computational power increases the chances of being the first to solve the puzzle, but does not guarantee it.

Bitcoin, for example, employs a hash-based proof of work which requires finding a “nonce” value. When combined with other variables, the resultant value of the hash has to be less than the target value. When such a nonce is found, the miner creates the block and forwards it to other participants for verification and adoption. Once other participants have verified the solution, they accept the new block and work on solving the next crypto-problem to gain the right to mine the next block (Gervais et. al., 2016).

The majority decision made through the proof of work is represented by the longest chain, as discussed in Section 4.2. The longest chain is representation of the greatest “proof of work” invested in it. When a majority of processing power is controlled by the honest nodes, the honest chains will grow faster and outpace the alternative chain being propagated by malicious nodes. Moreover, to modify a past block, the attacker will not only have to redo the block they are interested in, but all the subsequent blocks. That is the attacker node will have to regenerate the proof of work of that particular block and every subsequent block and then catch up and surpass the work that has been performed by the honest nodes in the meantime. This concept is illustrated in [Fig. 4.7](#CH004.html#fig7). Thus, the attacker should have computation power that surpasses the combined computation power of the honest nodes.

**Fig. 4.7:    Proof of Work Makes Alterations Difficult in Blockchain.[²](#CH004.html#fn2)**

![image](images/C04-FIG7.jpg)

While the longest chain, at any given point, is propagated and survives, it is important to note that there could be “stale blocks.” Stale blocks refer to blocks that were mined but are not included in the longest chain, instead they are part of a defunct fork. These are detrimental to the security and performance of the entire blockchain, as they trigger chain forks. The chain forks consume computational time and energy away from the main chain, thereby slowing down the growth of the main chain. Additionally, they impose security risks as malicious nodes could take advantage of the stale blocks to compromise the network.

**Fig. 4.8:    The Sudoku Puzzle.**

![image](images/C04-FIG8.jpg)

The cryptographic complex problem that needs to be solved by the miners can be solved only through brute force. It requires finding a solution by iteratively trying out all possible combinations. Thus, probabilistically the miner with the most computational power will have the greater chance of being the first to solve the puzzle. The puzzle has the following two properties:

*   The problem is hard to solve.
    
*   Once attained, the solution can be easily verified.
    

To better understand how the above rules work, consider the Sudoku puzzle shown in [Fig. 4.8](#CH004.html#fig8). For those unfamiliar with the game of Sudoku, the aim is to fill each row, column and the nine boxes with numbers 1–9, such that no number is repeated in the row, column or the box. The numbers already given in the cell cannot be altered. Suppose, this is the puzzle to be solved and the fastest to the correct solution gets to build the next block and collect coins for her efforts.

Those who are quite proficient in solving Sudoku puzzles with logic will still take about 10 minutes to solve the above. Whereas, with excess computation power, the computer would solve it through brute force, randomly assigning numbers to the cells, until the correct solution is found. More importantly, while it will take about 10 minutes for proficient problem solvers to find the solution, it will take less than a minute for anyone, with a basic understanding of numbers, to verify the solution to this puzzle shown in [Fig. 4.9](#CH004.html#fig9).

**Fig. 4.9:    Solution to the Sudoku Puzzle.**

![image](images/C04-FIG9.jpg)

This is why it is so difficult to tamper with existing blocks on blockchain. Even for a simple puzzle like Sudoku, a miscreant would have to solve many of these individually, whereas others are collectively solving additional puzzles. Referring back to the example shown in [Fig. 4.7](#CH004.html#fig7), the miscreant has to solve 16 puzzles, one for each block from #75 to #90, while others are working on puzzle #91. Let’s assume that the miscreant is actually twice as faster than the collective group. Even then, by the time it takes the miscreant to solve 16 puzzles, the group conservatively would have solved at least eight more and would be on puzzle #99. Now for the miscreant to solve these eight puzzles, the group would have solved four more, and so on. Perhaps, after solving 32 puzzles, or by puzzle #106, the miscreant would have caught up with the group. Instead of expending resources to recreate blocks from #74 onwards, the miscreant could have legitimately been the first to solve the puzzles from #91 to #106 and collect the reward. If the system provided substantial reward for solving the new puzzles, it may not be in the economic interest of the miscreant to change block #74, and would instead opt for legitimately creating new blocks and collecting corresponding rewards.

The proof of work model is a security measure that combines incentives with distributed computing and market economics. This innovation has empowered blockchain to function with secure and distributed consensus building system that has proven successful for over a decade. The limitation of proof of work is its reliance on computational power and its energy-intensive design. For example, in the case of Bitcoin, the balancing of costs of energy expenditure with its value is a convoluted task.

### 4.6. PROOF OF STAKE

There is an alternate consensus mechanism that does not need the energy or computational requirement of the proof of work mechanism. Instead of solving a complex problem, as required in the proof of work, the proof of stake randomly selects one of the participants with a probability proportional to the stake that the participant possesses in the blockchain. Stake is referred to the amount of currency or interest that a participant is willing to “lock up” for a certain amount of time. The “lock up” is similar to putting up a collateral for a specified amount of time. In return for committing funds to the system, the participant gets a higher chance of winning the lottery, and the right to form the next block. Currently, the proof of stake is being used in existing virtual coin such as Nxt and Blackcoin.

As noted above, the selection of the next block builder is decided by a lottery, with each participant having a chance proportional to their stake in the network. This is similar to the NBA[³](#CH004.html#fn3) draft lottery. The team with the worst record has 16 tickets in the lottery, the team with the second worst record has 15 tickets, and so on. Since the lottery is limited to teams not making the playoffs, only 16 teams are eligible. Thus, the team that just missed the playoff has only one ticket. Overall, there are 136 tickets in the lottery, and the worst team having 16 tickets has about 12% chance of winning the lottery. The probabilities range from 1% to 12%, but if one has more tickets, they have a higher likelihood of winning the lottery.

Similar to the proof of work mechanism, the proof of stake also ensures the integrity of the system and the impossibility of changing a prior block. The escrow of tokens by each participant enables the selection of the next individual to build a block. It is difficult, if not impossible, for one individual to invest enough number of tokens to outpace the collective network. Moreover, if an individual has invested in that many tokens in the network, then it is in their best economic interest to preserve the value of the network, as they are heavily vested in the value and reputation of the network.

There are other consensus building mechanisms being trialed. One such mechanism is the proof of elapsed time, which needs node to computationally wait for a period of time before the next block is added to the blockchain. The compulsory wait time makes it computationally difficult to add blocks to the blockchain.

Another is a variation of the proof of stake model, known as the delegate proof of stake. In this mechanism the stakeholders use their stake as weight in a voting process to elect delegates, called witnesses. The witnesses, in turn, verify and execute new transaction and have the responsibility of appending these to the blocks. This is tantamount to election of Board of Directors in corporate governance. The majority shareholders have a greater say on who should serve on the Board, but at the same time have to nominate someone credible. Once nominated, the Directors act on their own volition. Similarly, the top witnesses in this mechanism are the ones who have collected the most votes, and earn the right to verify and execute transactions. The votes for witnesses by the stakeholders are weighed in proportion to their stake in the network.

### 4.7. CONCLUSION

The consensus mechanism, discussed in this chapter, is an essential feature of permissionless blockchains. The consensus mechanism generates trust between unknown individuals who cannot trust each other, hence would not transact with each other in the absence of a trusted third party. The consensus mechanism enables the blockchain framework to substitute for the trusted third party in peer-to-peer transactions, thus replacing traditional trusted third parties, such as lawyers, banks, agents and brokers.

In permissioned blockchain, on the other hand, where all the participants are known and trusted the consensus mechanism is not as critical. Transactions between known and trusted parties can occur even without a robust consensus mechanism, as the participants in permissioned blockchain have an inbuilt incentive to maintain the integrity of the blockchain network. A breach of the networks’ credibility will not only harm the participants financially but also would lead to a loss of their personal credibility. Thus a robust consensus mechanism is not a critical feature in permissioned blockchain.

Of all the features of the blockchain, the consensus mechanism is the most technical and is based on cryptographic proof as well as computational prowess. Obtaining a technical understanding of the consensus mechanisms, such as in proof of work, requires training in mathematics and computer science. Moreover, since it also deals with incentives in a group setting with independent actors, a familiarity with the discipline of game theory[⁴](#CH004.html#fn4) is also needed. In this chapter, we have presented a non-technical appreciation of the concept. A basic familiarity with the concept is essential to understand the functioning of various features of the blockchain as well as its applications.

For permissionless blockchains to prosper and grow, the underlying consensus mechanism has to gain universal trust. One of the key advantages of blockchain infrastructure is its immutability, in that the records stored in the blockchain cannot be altered and is preserved forever. The consensus mechanism, making it difficult if not impossible to alter previous blocks, leads credence to the immutability feature inherent in blockchains.

Currently, the proof of work is the widely accepted consensus mechanism in blockchains. However, other consensus mechanisms, such as proof of stake or proof of elapsed time, are being developed and tested. The proof of work mechanism requires much computational power and energy (gas) to operate. Finding an easier and cheaper alternative will hasten the growth and acceptance of blockchain-based substitute of traditional and established recordkeeping processes.

Subsequent chapters discuss many applications of blockchain and in all those applications the consensus mechanism is relied upon to provide the trust and immutability of the records. There are of course certain limitations and vulnerabilities to the consensus mechanism which will be discussed in subsequent chapters. Current research in computer science and security are exploring ways to make the consensus mechanisms even more robust and less vulnerable to malicious attacks.
