## 9

## A TRANSPARENT NEW WORLD: ETHICALLY SOURCED MINERAL SUPPLY CHAIN

NATHAN WILLIAMS AND DAVID LEE WILLIAMS

### 9.1. INTRODUCTION

The good management of global business activities has become more challenging as society advances in its understanding of the impact of our activities on each other and on our planet. Business activities that a generation ago were unfettered, undocumented or unregulated are now becoming better understood, scrutinized, documented and regulated, and the consequences of undesirable activities are now much more visible. The term “responsible sourcing” has been used to describe the desire to assure sourcing that doesn’t compromise human values (such as using child labor or forced labor), harm society (such as supporting illegal or criminal activity, or forcing land resettlement) or negatively impact the environment (such as causing deforestation or chemical pollution).

*Responsible sourcing, also referred to as supply chain responsibility, is a voluntary commitment by companies to take into account social and environmental considerations when managing their relationships with suppliers.[¹](#CH009.html#fn1)*

Businesses need to be aware of the behavior of the participants in their particular supply chains in order to be able to assure responsible sourcing, however that knowledge can be hampered by a need to protect business confidentiality, by a lack of knowledge of the participants more than one transaction removed from the company, and until recently by a lack of concern with events outside the walls of the company. A recent increase in regulatory request, consumer interest and potential legal liability have moved enterprises to expand the role of product sourcing beyond a simple interaction with their immediate suppliers. A company’s procurement service that once would have been limited to such immediate needs as identifying reliable suppliers, evaluating product quality and ensuring timely delivery, now needs to seek assistance from other departments within the company to manage new purchasing subjects such as ensuring regulatory compliance, understanding and avoiding harmful business risk, and even previously unimagined challenges such as avoiding participation in illegal activity, money laundering and human slavery – in other words, in providing due diligence; due diligence being the care that a reasonable person exercises to avoid harm to other persons or their property.

The scope of these purchasing activities is expanding from the evaluation of one’s immediate suppliers, to now considering the full supply chain and assuring due diligence from suppliers several steps preceding the immediate supplier, that is, to entities with whom the business may have no formal relationship or contact.

### 9.2. COMPLEXITY OF MINERAL SUPPLY CHAIN

The mineral supply chain consists of several sequential processes or links. Each process involves multiple participants, and multiple activities. It is evident that performing due diligence in the mineral supply chain is not easy. A good example of the complexity of the mineral supply chain can be seen in the following graphic illustration of the gold supply chain.

![image](images/C09-UFIG1.jpg)

The gold supply chain consists of a series of general processes or links, involving: tens of countries where the mineral awaits; millions of miners, both artisanal and industrial; thousands of local traders and exporters; hundreds of smelters in different countries; thousands of traders of finished raw material; thousands of component manufacturers; tens of thousands of manufacturers of the finished product; and hundreds of thousands of different finished products to be sold to the final consumers.

![image](images/C09-UFIG2.jpg)

The first process in the gold supply chain is the government of the country where the gold is located. Each country regulates the ability to mine and ownership of mines, as well as the human, environmental, safety and political conditions under which mining can occur. Countries may cooperate with international regulation and guidance or may govern independently. Countries, just like individuals, can be responsible or corrupt, laissez-faire or interventionist, nationalistic or global in their behaviors, and this variability means that some countries are much less effective than others in assuring responsible activities in the gold supply chain. Countries also vary in their permission or prohibition of artisanal mining, individual land ownership, and mining rights.

![image](images/C09-UFIG3.jpg)

To get the gold ore out of the ground requires miners. Estimates say 100 million people depend on artisanal mining. About seven million people are employed in large-scale industrial mining (LSM) worldwide. Artisanal and Small-Scale Mining (ASM) occurs in approximately 80 countries. ASM production supply accounts for 20% of mined gold. A typical ASM mining process includes digging the gold ore, transporting the ore (carried in bags or by wheelbarrow) to where it is manually crushed, washed and filtered. The filtered sediment is processed chemically to yield an amalgam that is then sold to local traders. LSM extraction of gold typically involves machine crushing and milling the rock to a powder or slurry, then vat or heap leaching using sodium cyanide to yield impure gold.

![image](images/C09-UFIG4.jpg)

There are a variety of forms of transportation involved in moving gold ore from the mine site to the smelter. In the instance of ASM the gold amalgam needs to be assembled in sufficient quantity to be exported to smelters. This involves transport by a variety of means: from bicycle and motor scooter, to truck and trailer, and to private airplane. Road transport may be subject to arbitrary taxation by local government officials or extortion by illegal groups. Air transport may even be untraceable (see Section 9.5).

![image](images/C09-UFIG5.jpg)

Local traders may purchase gold amalgam from miners and sell it in turn to large traders, or miners may act as a cooperative and sell to large traders directly. Large traders may then smelt the gold themselves or may ship the amalgam to a smelter. Other participants in the process between the miner and the smelter may include mine supervisors, local tribal authority, government officials, police and others, each of whom can perform their work legally or illegally.

![image](images/C09-UFIG6.jpg)

The smelter is considered the midpoint in the mineral supply chain. Smelters receive not only mineral ore from LSM and ASM but also recycled material coming from a variety of sources. In a smelter, gold is released from its natural mineral state by melting and then reacting with chemicals. The resulting doré gold is a semi-pure alloy of gold and silver that can vary in purity, so it is subsequently then transported to refineries, where further treatment can bring the purity of the gold to nearly 100%.

![image](images/C09-UFIG7.jpg)

A component manufacturer will purchase gold from metal trader who has purchased the processed gold from a smelter or a refiner. The component manufacturer may need only minute amounts of gold for each component but will be making millions of components. Gold is a very good electrical conductor, it does not easily corrode in air, and is ductile. For these reasons, gold is the preferred material used to make small electrical connectors, circuit traces, integrated circuits, transistors and memory chips.

![image](images/C09-UFIG8.jpg)

Finished product manufacturers are the final immediate process in our gold supply chain. Our modern electronics are complex assemblages of dozens of small components, each of which contains gold. In fact, virtually any electronic appliance that has a circuit board, from old TVs and VCRs to GPS systems and solar panels, will contain gold.

![image](images/C09-UFIG9.jpg)

The final process in the gold supply chain is recycling. Thirty-five to 40 smartphones (without batteries) contain roughly 1 gram of gold, the same quantity that appears in a ton of lower grade gold ore in the ground. Recycled gold appears at the midpoint of the chain at the smelter, where it is reprocessed along with new ore to make doré gold.

Regulation and guidelines have so far focused their attention on the most apparent participants in the supply chain for consumer goods, namely, the finished product manufacturers, yet the same motivation for regulation (assurance of safe process and prevention of unwanted consequence) can be applied the length of the supply chain. Given the complexity, tools are needed to provide transparency and consistency to all processes in a mineral supply chain while maintaining business confidentiality and encouraging fair business competition.

### 9.3. LACK OF TRANSPARENCY IN MINERAL SUPPLY CHAIN

![image](images/C09-UFIG10.jpg)

Each of the participants in a process link in the supply chain is exceedingly hard-pressed to know the multiple members of the processes upstream from their immediate suppliers.

![image](images/C09-UFIG11.jpg)

A component manufacturer will know from which of hundreds of metal traders they sourced their minerals from, but is unlikely (for business confidentiality reasons among others) to have any idea which of tens of thousands of providers of local mineral transport were used to get the mineral to which trading houses for amassing and transporting then to which smelters for processing. These logistical challenges are indeed complex.

According to Dynda A. Thomas, partner with Squire Patton Boggs,[²](#CH009.html#fn2)

*There are roughly 400 smelters and refiners of the processed conflict minerals world-wide. Because of that limited number of processors, industry is able to focus directly on requiring them to comply with recognized due diligence standards for conflict minerals sourced from the Democratic Republic of Congo and adjoining countries. So, most companies have not undertaken extraordinary efforts to determine the particular smelters and refiners of the processed conflict minerals in their products. Instead most have opted to identify all the possible smelters and refiners in all their supply chains.*

### 9.4. EVOLVING REGULATION ON TRACEABILITY OF MINERALS

The individual sectors of the mining supply chain have historically been regulated by national governments. Mines have been regulated by mining rules, smelters by industry-specific health, safety and environment laws, etc. Until recently the mining supply chain has not been the focus of international regulation on process and consequence. The current decade is witnessing the implementation of regulation that directly impacts the mineral supply chain. This activity has been driven by rising government and consumer awareness of unethical practices that are being used in the provision of mineral resources.

#### 9.4.1. Conflict Minerals Regulations

Businesses involved in the mineral supply chain in 2019 need to know their suppliers. For certain minerals, namely tungsten, tin, tantalum and gold (the 3TG minerals) they need to know their suppliers specifically because they are regulated to do so. These minerals are mined in some countries using child labor and forced labor. The proceeds of this mining frequently support illegal activities and/or military insurgents, and the initial conflict mineral regulations were meant to impact the purchase of such illegal minerals and reduce human rights abuse. Reporting obligations for enterprises in the mineral supply chain have evolved in seven years since the first Act.

*   *The Dodd-Frank Act (2012)*[³](#CH009.html#fn3) was the first regulation to apply the concept of corporate supply chain responsibility to the mineral supply chain for 3TG minerals. The Act requested documentation however did not prescribe penalties for non-compliance.
    
*   *The OECD Due Diligence Guidance for Responsible Supply Chains*[⁴](#CH009.html#fn4) of Minerals from Conflict-Affected and High-Risk Areas (CAHRAs) issued in 2011 and amended in 2012 recommended measures to integrate due diligence into corporate management systems.
    
*   *The Chinese GSRM (2014)* expressed guidance in social responsibility and sustainable development for Chinese companies operating abroad.
    
*   *The Chinese Due Diligence Guidelines for Responsible Mineral Supply Chains (2015)*[⁵](#CH009.html#fn5) provided guidance for Chinese companies to identify, prevent and mitigate their risks of contributing to conflict, serious human rights abuses and risks of serious misconduct.
    
*   *The third edition OECD Due Diligence Guidance (2016)*[⁶](#CH009.html#fn6) articulated recommendations on supply chain due diligence and described a duty to report.
    
*   *The EU Conflict Minerals Regulation (2017)*[⁷](#CH009.html#fn7) goes a step further by addressing the concept of corporate social liability and the risk of harm. Corporations are now seen to have a social liability within their sphere of influence for the social, ecological and economic consequences of the company’s activities. Reporting needs to be transparent to enable others to monitor and verify how a company is meeting its duties and trying to mitigate problems. The EU Conflict Minerals Regulation does envisage future consequences for non-compliance. The bar has been raised for reporting in the mineral supply chain.
    
*   *The Mosi-oa-Tunya Declaration*[⁸](#CH009.html#fn8) was adopted on September 13, 2018 at the International Conference on Artisanal and Small-scale Mining and Quarrying (ASM18) in Livingstone, Zambia. This declaration called on government and international private sector to ensure that production and sourcing practices do not contribute to adverse human rights or to conflict and its financing.
    
*   *EU Non-binding Guidelines for identifying CAHRAs*[⁹](#CH009.html#fn9) Commission Recommendation (EU) 2018/1149 was published on August 10, 2018. These guidelines will aid companies understand the definition of CAHRAs as described in the EU Conflict Minerals Regulation. In addition to providing suggested open source resources for companies to use in identifying CAHRAs the guidelines provide a series of “red flag” topics. Red flags are situations that “give rise to a reasonable suspicion that the minerals may contribute to conflict or serious abuses associated with their extraction, transport or trade.”
    

The overall objective of these regulations has been to initiate the establishment of secure, transparent and verifiable supply chains of minerals and to ensure responsible practices, while not contributing to armed conflict and associated human rights abuse.

#### 9.4.2. Modern Slavery and Human Rights Regulations

In the past seven years, two international organizations, three governments and one US state have introduced regulations or directives concerning the assessment of modern slavery and human rights abuse in the workforce. In their protocols, industry is required to perform risk assessment of their direct business as well as that of their suppliers, and to report on those assessments annually.

*   *The California Transparency in Supply Chains Act*[^(10)](#CH009.html#fn10) went into effect in 2012. It requires medium and larger manufacturing and retail businesses generating global revenue exceeding $100 million to disclose their initiatives to remove human trafficking and slavery from their direct supply chains. The intent of the law is to provide consumers with information on which companies responsibly manage their supply chains.
    
*   *The EU Directive on Non-Financial Disclosures (2014)*[^(11)](#CH009.html#fn11) are non-compulsory guidelines intended to help companies disclose information on various non-financial aspects of their operations in their annual reports. Companies have flexibility in what they report, on subjects including policies, risks and due diligence implemented and on issues such as anti-corruption and bribery issues, environmental, social and employee matters.
    
*   *The United Nations Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights (UNGPs)*[^(12)](#CH009.html#fn12) were endorsed by the UN Human Rights Council in June 2011. They provided the first global standard for addressing the risk of adverse impacts on human rights linked to business activity, The UNGPs identify three fundamentals: the state duty to protect human rights; the corporate responsibility to respect human rights; and access to remedy for victims of business-related abuses
    
*   *The UK Modern Slavery Act (2015)*[^(13)](#CH009.html#fn13) makes companies accountable for slavery and labor abuses occurring along their whole chain of operations. Any business – or part of a business – that has a global turnover of £36m or more and supplies goods or services in the UK must produce and publish an annual slavery and trafficking statement. The statement must set out what steps the organization has taken to ensure there is no slavery in any part of its business, including its supply chains.
    
*   *Australian Modern Slavery Act (2018)*[^(14)](#CH009.html#fn14) Australia’s Modern Slavery Act, like the UK’s requires businesses and other organizations (including government departments) above a certain size (consolidated revenue of A$100 million) to report annually on the risks of modern slavery in their operations and supply chains, and the action they have taken to assess and address those risks, and the effectiveness of their response.
    
*   *French Corporate Duty of Vigilance Law (2017)*[^(15)](#CH009.html#fn15) Companies subject to this law, their subsidiaries, and companies under their control, must establish mechanisms to prevent human rights violations and environmental impacts throughout their chain of production. These mechanisms must be reported each year as part of a “vigilance plan.” The vigilance plan should identify and analyze the risks of human rights violation or environmental harms, identify actions to mitigate or prevent the most serious violations, and discuss the effectiveness of those actions.
    

The evolution of global obligation to analyze, action, measure effectiveness and report on measures taken to address the risk of modern slavery in the supply chain have somewhat paralleled the activity suggested or required by conflict minerals regulation. In both cases, reporting requirements or guidelines imply corporate understanding of the components of their supply chain, and thus imply a transparency to the participants in the supply chain that to this date are not present. Regulations noted above, even the most current ones, are strong in guidance but weak in consequence. The Independent Review of the Modern Slavery Act Section 54 concerning transparency in supply chains notes:

*\[…\] there is a general agreement between businesses and civil society that a lack of enforcement and penalties, as well as confusion surrounding reporting obligations, are core reasons for poor-quality statements and the estimated lack of compliance from over a third of eligible firms.[^(16)](#CH009.html#fn16)*

#### 9.4.3. Ramifications of Changing Political Dynamics

In 2017, the US House approved an amendment to a spending bill and that amendment cut off funding to “implement, administer, or enforce” the Dodd-Frank Conflict Minerals Rule. What had commenced as a recognition by Congress of the significant problem of illegal activity in the global provision of minerals was virtually rescinded in less than a decade.

However, industry recognizes the significance of the intent of the regulations. Some large companies voluntarily comply with the previous legislation, having put the processes and controls in place. For example, one large multinational food company, Nestlé, implemented their own Nestlé Responsible Sourcing Standard[^(17)](#CH009.html#fn17) in July 2018. The standard defines expected duties, practices and reporting by third-party suppliers and establishes milestones for improvement. Another global technology company, Intel, writes in 2019,

*While we do not procure direct, our suppliers acquire and use minerals from multiple sources worldwide. Intel is committed to the responsible sourcing of minerals – sourcing done in an ethical and sustainable manner that safeguards the human rights of everyone in our global supply chain …. Prompted by growing concerns about child labor and additional human rights violations beyond the scope of Dodd-Frank, Intel has evolved its responsible minerals program and related due diligence practices to address minerals originating from CAHRAs.[^(18)](#CH009.html#fn18)*

### 9.5. MONEY LAUNDERING THROUGH MINERAL SOURCING

Money laundering is the act of disguising the source or true nature of money obtained through illegal means.[^(19)](#CH009.html#fn19) The money to be “laundered” may come from any number of illegal activities, including illegal arms sales, smuggling, drug trafficking, prostitution, embezzlement, insider trading, bribery, computer fraud schemes or, in the context of this discussion, illegal mining. Money obtained in these ways is considered “dirty,” as it would appear suspicious if large quantities of money simply appeared in an individual’s bank account. This money is “cleaned,” by passing through several “legitimate” businesses before being deposited, thus obscuring its true origin. With the origin obscured, the “cleaned” money will appear to have been derived from legal activities, with the result that financial institutions handle it without suspicion.

There is considerable incentive for illegal activity in gold: gold is fungible (one sample of gold appears identical to any other sample of gold); gold is of high value per mass making it relatively easy to smuggle; and gold is universally traded and exchanged. A recent example of the extent of the use of gold to launder money or gold laundering appeared in the Americas in 2016. Three ex-employees of an American metal refining company along with accomplices from several South American countries purchased rough gold from illegal gold mines owned by drug traffickers in Peru. The gold was smelted into doré bars which were then transported via Ecuador, Bolivia, Colombia and other Latin American countries by air to a refinery in the United States. The refined gold of course had the same composition as gold refined from any other source, save it was illegal gold mined at the expense of forced labor and possibly child labor from illegal drug cartel-owned mines hidden in the Amazon forest. These traders succeeded in laundering $3.6 billion worth of gold between 2013 and 2016 before being arrested.

Traditional sourcing methods are hard-pressed to identify the presence or absence of illegal gold in a refined bar. New methods are needed to assure to identify the “responsibly obtained” minerals and to distinguish them from those obtained illicitly, under duress, or as a component of illegal financial activity.

### 9.6. TRACEABILITY REQUIREMENTS

The OECD Due Diligence Guidance recommends that companies generate several types of information, the specificities of which depending upon the placement of the source along the processes in the mineral supply chain. This data should be gathered for locations of mineral origin as well as transit. Specifically, the guidance recommends obtaining data for minerals:

*   originating from or transported via a conflict-affected or high-risk area;
    
*   originating from a country having limited known reserves; or
    
*   originating from a country through which CAHRA minerals are known to transit.
    

Supplier data should be gathered for companies having shareholder or other interests in companies that supply or have supplied minerals from or operate in a CAHRA origin or transit country.

Trader data should be gathered, recording all payments given for export, import and re-export, as well as all taxes and payments made to government and private security officials. This data is not the conventional data required in a simple supplier–client business exchange, and gathering this data calls for new innovation to assure confidentiality while maintaining transparency.

There are significant challenges to current methods of supply chain tracking. Properly knowing the supply chain history requires intense individual effort and communication with suppliers and their suppliers, and immense computational work to accurately identify all relevant elements. Supply chain participants are understandably reluctant to share sensitive data with entities that they may not know or trust, and participants are required to cooperate with what would be to them, unknown entities that they may be reluctant to trust. Responsible supply chain traceability may be seen as an essential business function with respect to the regulatory affairs or shareholder relations aspects of a business, but to other departments, may not be seen as essential to core business and meriting corporate resources. The traditional methods of supply chain audit and analysis depend on a significant paper trail, and paper certification provides neither strong traceability nor freedom from corruption. For these reasons, the present system of mineral supply chain tracking needs a new solution.

In 2014, in response to Dodd-Frank, downstream manufacturing companies took up the monumental task of attempting to track the origins of the materials in their supply chain. They spent over 700 million dollars tracking their products upstream in an attempt to identify the smelters and refiners supplying them. It was an expensive and lengthy process.

### 9.7. BLOCKCHAIN SOLUTION

As we can see the mineral supply chain is full of complex problems that have multifaceted causes. Blockchain, paired with careful system design, provides us with a way to address these problems. Blockchain has four key characteristics that make it suitable to address these increasing regulatory challenges.

The first two characteristics are the tools of our trade. They allow us to create a system that can keep track of the history, or provenance of the materials we are transacting and ensure that record is trustworthy:

**1.** Blockchain can *tokenize* key due diligence data on tracked minerals, submitted by all participating supply chain actors. These tokens are unique digital assets that resist tampering and duplication.

**2.** Blockchain maintains an *immutable* of transactions. This guards against the changing of documents, which is the most common vector for corruption and supply chain risk.

The other two characteristics provide the incentives for every supply chain actor to use our well-designed tools. They are how we can turn our system from an interesting tool for companies who know each other well into a global infrastructure that can address some of the deepest problems the mineral supply chain faces:

**3.** Blockchain can create self-governed *incentive systems*, to make it beneficial for supply chain actors to participate in the system.

**4.** Blockchain allows parties who do not trust each other to *transact without a trusted third party*, eliminating the need to trust a single company with supply chain data.

The success of our mineral blockchain tracking system depends completely on designing the tools and the incentives properly.

#### 9.7.1. Recording the History of Supply Chain

Blockchain enables us to create digital assets such as tokens that cannot be duplicated or altered. We leverage this trait to limit the number of tokens available, and this uniqueness allows the representation of real world assets such as minerals or metals. Once we have a token representation of our minerals, we can link due diligence data to them and keep track of the history of who held them, giving us a powerful tool for supply chain security.

Tokens can be created that are either fungible or non-fungible depending on the assets we would like to tokenize. There are many ways we can make use of this digital uniqueness to ensure responsibility in our supply chain, each with strengths and weaknesses depending on the specific problem to be addressed.

Let’s start by looking at fungible tokens. With fungible tokens we only keep track of the quantity we have; every token we purchase can be merged into a larger pile of tokens making them all indistinguishable from each other. Fungible tokens are the most commonly created type of token in the blockchain world. They are easy to handle, like a currency, can be stored in a wallet as a number, and moved around like cash. Metals are also fungible so it seems intuitive that a fungible token would work for tracking responsible materials.

#### 9.7.2. Fungible Tokens

Consider a company that wants to produce responsible gold for investment purposes. They purchase all their gold from one large-scale mine that links to one large-scale smelter, they have their production practices audited and show this information on a website or in a database. They process the gold into 1kg bars and put the bars into a vault. For each kilo bar in the vault they create a fungible, digital token on a blockchain which are then sold on the open market. The gold kilo token would contain a link back to the website or database, showing the percentage of responsible gold contained in that token.

There are many advantages to this approach. These are listed as follows:

*   Fungible tokens are easy to manage because we do not need a physical identifier linking the token to the metal. We know all the metal is in a vault, and it all comes from one source, so it doesn’t matter which bar is which.
    
*   Investors can move into and out of gold positions fluidly with minimal transaction fees.
    
*   Implementation is straightforward – there are few processes that would have to be changed, and only one company needs to know how to use the blockchain. One company can decide to do this unilaterally without the buy-in from every member of the supply chain.
    

There are some limitations as well, these are:

*   The tokens are fungible only because we control all the aspects of responsibility off the supply chain and keep the gold from these processes separate from the rest of the market. It works well when we have *one vault* storing all our gold coming from only known sources and audited supply chains. If, however, multiple companies each create their own vault and their own fungible token, these tokens could not be mixed without losing the information about which vaults held our gold. This makes such a system difficult to scale.
    
*   We need to ensure good data is provided to the blockchain. This example assumes the gold in the vault is responsible and the company is providing some data on it. This is possible when the gold is purchased directly from a mine with rigorous, documented processes in a very short supply chain, but most of the gold supply in the world does not meet these criteria, making this approach impossible to scale.
    
*   Fungible tokens may represent responsibly sourced metals, but they are still very valuable digital assets that can be transferred at will between wallets without needing a middleman. Ironically, this means the tokens can be used to launder money easily and we will need to build in KYC into such a system to address this.
    

If we want to have a greater level of detail over which metals come from where, we can use non-fungible tokens that represent metal in our supply chain. Non-fungible tokens can be created to represent specific shipments of metal ores or ingots, effectively acting as a digital twin that can be used to demonstrate who owns it. Each token can contain a link to data that demonstrates the responsibility of the shipment that it is linked to.

#### 9.7.3. Non-fungible Tokens

Some of the previous limitations can be addressed through the use of a non-fungible token. It is a bit like passing a baton from person to person in a relay race. At the end it will have the fingerprints of everyone who held it. To illustrate, let’s expand the above example by starting at a gold mine. Our responsible gold mine wants to sell responsibly sourced gold to their customers. They extract gold, melt it into “doré” bars that are weighed, and a non-fungible “doré” token is created to represent each bar. The doré token contains a unique ID, which is put on a tag and attached to the doré bar, as well as a link to a database of due diligence data. As the doré bars are passed from owner to owner, the associated non-fungible tokens are also passed along to each new owner. When the doré bars reaches the refiner, it is processed into 1kg gold bars and the tokens are frozen so they can no longer be traded. We issue new tokens for the new, refined gold kilo bars that contain a unique ID and a link to the old token. The gold kilo bars are put into a vault as before, and people purchasing or trading the new gold token have a record of its supply chain history.

Non-fungible tokens have advantages over fungible tokens, these are:

*   Non-fungible tokens allow us to represent individual units of material (the doré bars and the kilo bars). Having this extra granularity of data means we are not limited to a single mineral source or vault.
    
*   Because the individual shipments are represented, we can also record who held them and where they were produced. This allows us to track minerals from the point of production, all the way down the supply chain.
    
*   The tokens are linked to specific bars or shipments, making them more difficult to use for money laundering.
    

However, these also impose new challenges as follows:

*   For the tokens to track the metal, we need to *link the metal to the non-fungible tokens*. Tagging presents a challenge whenever the material is processed or changes form.
    
*   Because we are tracking materials along a supply chain, *we need all companies along the supply chain to participate*, making it more challenging to implement a project. These companies will likely need to adjust their internal processes to keep track of these blockchain-tracked shipments, meaning additional work and cost.
    
*   *We still need to ensure that good data is provided to the blockchain*. Our non-fungible tokens are only as useful as the data they are based on.
    

Non-fungible tokens are an intuitive way of tracking material shipments because in many ways they act like the physical materials they represent. As long as a company holds a token, they can prove the history of the associated metal.

Moreover, an additional improvement could be made for compliance purposes. After a company sells their products and transfers the associated non-fungible token, they still need to show that they used to own it, and that it was responsibly sourced. If we were using a regular traditional database, this would not be an issue at all. We would keep a record of the tokens we possessed, and their history and the compliance records can be prepared. Since we need this record secured and immutable, we need to store the information on the blockchain.

There are many ways to approach this, but one simple possibility is to put in a rule that non-fungible tokens can only be traded once and then they are frozen forever. Holding a non-fungible token allows one to create a new token that can also be traded once. This is a bit like passing a ball of yarn from person to person, with everyone still holding the thread after they’ve passed the ball.

#### 9.7.4. Operationalizing Tokenization

One of the primary challenges is to link the metal to the tokens. This is the number one question people ask when they think about supply chain solutions. How do you make sure the materials you are tracking match the records, certificates or tokens on the blockchain? When we say this, there are two functions we need to be able to perform:

**1.** If we have a shipment of material, we should be able to locate the blockchain token that shows us the record of ownership and associated due diligence data.

**2.** If we have a token we want to be able to identify and verify the shipment it relates to.

To accomplish the first task, we need to have a tag with a unique identifier that links to the blockchain. To accomplish the second, we need information about the material stored with the token, including identifying marks, last known location, and total mass and grade tracked by the token.

One illustration of the implementation is to consider an ingot of tin. On the ingot is a sticker with a QR code. We scan the QR code and an app calls up our non-fungible token showing us all the previous owners. Another illustration is that we have a token for a pallet of tin ingots displayed in an app. Along with the token, the app has information about which warehouse the tin is stored in, how many ingots in the pallet, a unique identifier and a photo of what it looks like. We can then go to the warehouse and locate the pallet.

The method we use to link the material with the token can vary depending on which problems we are trying to solve. For example, we may have concerns that minerals from illicit sources are being mixed in with our shipments before the minerals are processed into metal. Minerals look like piles of rocks so if this is a concern, we might weigh the shipment before and after transport and spray the minerals with an identifying compound such as a rare radioactive isotope.

We may have concerns that tracked metal ingots in our supply chain are being lost, and that companies are replacing the ingots with fakes that have the same ID number. If this is our concern, we might take high resolution photographs of the ingots that show fine detailed cracks and lines in the metal to prove the bar is genuine. The important thing is that the token tracking system be flexible enough to accommodate different identification methods. This will ensure the system can adapt to the different challenges of different minerals at different stages of the supply chain.

#### 9.7.5. Mass Processing of Minerals

Minerals and metals are raw materials, not end products by themselves. They need to be processed to be useful. Ores need to be smelted into metal. Metals need to be refined and alloyed to become useful in our end products. Most smelters and refiners use a continuous process where ores from many sources are processed together. These processes make batch-by-batch tracing unfeasible. The world of the future may be one where all these processes are handled batch-by-batch, but for the time being, we need another way to trace beyond these processing points in the supply chain.

In order to overcome these points of processing, we can use a mass–balance approach. The concept of mass–balance traceability is that at processing points we track volumes of material going into the process and the volumes coming out, rather than tracking the metals themselves.

Consider a smelter purchases five one-ton shipments of tin. One ton from mine A and four from mine B. These mines are both tracked, and the smelter receives tokens from both. After processing we create a new token representing the tin ingots that is linked to all the old tokens. In this way, we consider all the tin to be 20% from mine A and 80% from mine B, with a link to the due diligence information from both mines. This also allows us to claim that the money paid for the ingots is traceable, 20% to mine A and 80% to mine B.

It is unreasonable to expect every mine to adopt a blockchain traceability system overnight. How can we deal with smelters using material from a combination of blockchain-tracked and non-blockchain sources? In this case, we can create non-fungible tokens at the smelter level, linked to due diligence data that the smelter has performed on their mines. These tokens would not give the same level of visibility as the tokens generated at the mine but would allow customers the choice of what to purchase.

In [Fig. 9.1](#CH009.html#fig1), we can see a smelter that has purchased 10 tons of material but only nine from blockchain-tracked mines. After processing, their customer wishes to purchase nine tons of blockchain-tracked metal. The smelter then sends nine tons of metal that has been mixed together from both sources, however by including the nine tons of blockchain-tracked certificates, the customer is able to know their money went to the blockchain-registered sources.

**Fig. 9.1:    Mass–Balance in the Mineral Supply Chain.**

![image](images/C09-FIG1.jpg)

*Source*: Minespider White Paper, [www.minespider.com](http://www.minespider.com).

#### 9.7.6. Greenwashing

It is important that whether all a smelter’s input mines are using blockchain or not, that they are all responsible. We do not want blockchain-based mineral traceability to be a vector for greenwashing. It is not enough for a smelter to purchase 50% from responsible blockchain-registered sources, and the other 50% from known conflict sources, selling their blockchain traceability tokens to downstream customers who care about traceability, and anonymous material to everyone else. This could provide a false sense of confidence to downstream companies that they are not the ones contributing to illicit activities, reduce pressure on negative actors in the supply chain, and ultimately make on-the-ground impacts worse than not using blockchain at all.

To avoid this, it is important that the smelters continue to perform their due diligence on their input mines as the OECD guidelines require and encourage more and more mines to register on the blockchain over time. Blockchain tokens should be created at the smelter level with the associated due diligence the smelter has done to account for any material from sources not already blockchain-enabled.

In the example above, the smelter purchased 10 tons of material, nine of which came with non-fungible tokens that allow us to trace all the way back to the mine of origin. For the one ton outstanding, a token should be created at the smelter level, linked to the due diligence the smelter has performed. Customers purchasing that material would be aware the token has not come from the mine of origin but would still have some level of trust and information on the material, and leverage for pushing the mines to adopt traceability going forward.

### 9.8. INCENTIVIZING PARTICIPATION IN BLOCKCHAIN

The best-designed mineral traceability system only tracks minerals if companies use it, and they will only use it if they see how they benefit. If the benefits outweigh the costs, companies will join and continue to participate. If not, the system becomes a hypothetical proof of concept.

One of the core features of blockchain is the ability to create systems and exchange value based on a shared set of rules. These rules are enforced by computers running the blockchain system, so no single user has power to change them. By setting these rules properly we can make sure every user in our traceability system sees a benefit from participating.

#### 9.8.1. Incentives for Companies

The more participants in a system, the harder it is to set rules that incentivize everyone. Rule-setting in a supply chain system can get very complex, but we can start with two simple rules:

**1.** If a participant provides value to the system, they get adequate compensation or some form of value for participating.

**2.** If a participant derives value out of the system, they must pay for it, or create value to the system.

If a company provides data to the blockchain, that is valuable. They should be rewarded in some way, such as money, increased reputation or better access to customers. If a company is using the blockchain for compliance they should pay for it in some way, such as by paying money, or setting up computers to help run the blockchain system.

We’ve already seen a slate of different actors in the supply chain. Let’s return to our earlier example of the gold supply chain to see possible benefits each participant could receive from using a blockchain traceability system, and of examples rules we might include to support that.

![image](images/C09-UFIG12.jpg)

![image](images/C09-UFIG13.jpg)

Governments in regions where gold is located may wish to control the extraction and transaction of gold within their borders, to prevent smuggling, funding of conflict groups, tax evasion, and human rights abuses. Governments may wish to require individuals and companies to sell through a government-endorsed channel that is registered with blockchain tracking. To encourage governments to adopt our system we can require country-specific licenses to transact with gold on our system as well as automated tax collection.

![image](images/C09-UFIG14.jpg)

Mines provide due diligence data to access the world market. Large-Scale Mining operations (LSM) undergo expensive audits and collect due diligence data to assure market players to purchase from them, and mines would like the cost of providing this data reduced. Artisanal Miners (ASM) generally cannot provide this data and would benefit from having legal channels through which to sell. This would provide this data for them that would be enforced by local government. In addition to the reputational incentive, we may also provide ASM with benefits such as better prices, money to build up infrastructure in their community, and digital payments to make compliance easier and more beneficial than those obtained through illegal sales.

![image](images/C09-UFIG15.jpg)

Transporters often have many documents they must produce to legally ship material. These documents might take days to obtain and keep sorted. By associating these documents with our shipment tokens, we provide an efficiency benefit.

Security is also a large concern with transporters, with high value shipments at risk of being robbed. We might include rules that delay recording data about transport on the token until after shipment arrives.

![image](images/C09-UFIG16.jpg)

Traders, smelters and manufacturers all have their own compliance needs as well as customers asking for supply chain information, and a need to access the world market. They all provide some value to the system whenever they trade tokens or upload relevant data, and they all benefit from receiving compliance information from the system. The balance of how much they benefit versus how much they contribute is still an open question that will likely be answered over time on a case-by-case basis.

![image](images/C09-UFIG17.jpg)

Finished product manufacturers often are the brand names who benefit the most from the supply chain transparency provided by the system. These actors would have detailed supply chain information and in return they should pay the most for it.

![image](images/C09-UFIG18.jpg)

Recyclers are the end point in the supply chain; however, they are also a point where gold starts over, feeding material into the smelters. Recyclers will provide data to our blockchain system showing the information they have on where their inputs came from, and as such should have a benefit to providing this data, in the form of a better reputation, or reduced costs of compliance.

#### 9.8.2. Data Self-sovereignty

Who gets to see the data that is attached to our tokens? This is a key question that will likely give many companies pause before joining a blockchain traceability program. Some companies’ business models are based on arbitrage, and supply chain transparency may cause them to lose business. Other companies may have legitimate anti-trust concerns about being in communication with their deeper tier suppliers or cooperating with their competitors for supply chain responsibility. For most companies these concerns will hold much more sway than the economic incentives of the system.

To illustrate, imagine a company making $20m per year trading minerals. It will not matter whether the blockchain system costs them $50,000 or pays them $50,000 if it threatens their core business of $20m per year. They will only join if it means a secured access to the global mineral market of the future, and only if their data is secure.

The sensitivity of supply chain data implies a guiding principle: all data in our traceability system should be under the control of the data owner and nobody else. This principle is known as data *self-sovereignty*. It means the owner of a blockchain token is the only one who has access to see the data about the minerals the token represents. No other company or government actor would be able to access that supply chain data. The token holder would be solely responsible for sharing their responsibility data at their own discretion. By holding to the principle of data self-sovereignty, we can overcome the strongest disincentive to companies becoming blockchain-registered responsible supply chain participants.

#### 9.8.3. Immutability of Data

Most corruption happens with money and payments and is hidden by changing documents after the fact. If records of mineral sales, provenance and due diligence are stored permanently and irrevocably along with our mineral traceability tokens, it closes the largest avenues for corruption and the illicit practices that grow from it. No supply chain actor wants to pay bribes to get business done. The promise of unchangeable records acts as a strong positive incentive to join for companies who want to be responsible. In the long run the industry demands for transparency in the mineral supply chain will only increase, and companies that benefit from corruption will be pushed out of the market, due to ill-reputation and their failure to engage in responsible tracking system. Immutable records are better for every actor in the supply chain but are especially useful for helping large-scale mines maintain their social contract to operate in countries with weaker institutional oversight.

In July 2017, Acacia Mines, a subsidiary of Barrick Gold Corporation, was presented with a $190 billion bill for unpaid taxes and fines from the government of Tanzania for operating illegally and under-declaring the value of their gold exports.[^(20)](#CH009.html#fn20) This bill was more than the $162 billion GDP of the country in 2017. Ultimately the situation was resolved with Barrick agreeing to pay $300 million and the government taking a 16% stake in Acacia Mines. Having an immutable record would have resolved this issue before it began.

#### 9.8.4. Ensuring Credible Information

Our token traceability system is only as useful as the data that is entered. If tokens are created for minerals with falsified due diligence reports, the credibility of the system will be damaged. If the data in our system is used to give companies access to the world market for their minerals, then companies have a strong incentive to have a positive standing. If mine, smelter, or other supply chain participant with shady practices must choose between cleaning up their practices and lying that they have, they will likely go with whichever is easier. Therefore, to incentivize good data in the blockchain we need to increase the difficulty to fake data.

The first thing we can do is require participating companies to undergo third-party audits and attach a scan of their reports to the non-fungible tokens. This is a good start, but how can we improve it? A third-party compliance audit may be paid for by the company undergoing the audit resulting in a conflict of interest in the final report. It results in a single point of failure in our system.

A more robust solution would be to create a standard set of data to be collected and signed off on by multiple parties at multiple points in the supply chain. Some data, such as shipment amounts and numbers, grade and location might be collected automatically from enterprise management software or internet of things devices in the production facilities. Automation makes it more difficult to fake data increasing trust.

By forming a standard set of data to be collected, we can then score companies based on the practices they employ, like a social reputation score. Companies who know their suppliers, tag and track their shipments, have good relationships to civil society, are transparent about worker wages, and send and receive all payments electronically might get a higher social reputation score than companies with fewer such practices. If customers prefer suppliers with a higher social reputation score, it could act as an incentive for companies to improve their practices and invest in data collection and tracking.

#### 9.8.5. Incentivizing Incident Reporting

Once companies are part of our system, we can have a degree of confidence in their practices from the fact that they are willing to participate in a transparency program. Nevertheless, transparency programs and responsibility policies might not mitigate all supply chain risks. Even a mine–transport–trader–smelter group who implements a robust and blockchain-backed responsibility program could experience problematic incidents. For example:

*   A truck might be robbed, and illegal taxes collected by a conflict group.
    
*   Workers in a more remote mine site may bring children to work in the mine and hide them during scheduled inspections.
    
*   Workers may dispose of hazardous waste improperly.
    

To address and reduce incidents of illicit behavior, we first need these incidents reported and these reports attached to our tokens. We need to identify a group that is in a position to witness the incident, identify how to make reporting easy, identify incentives that would encourage them to make the report, and then identify how to prevent false reports.

Incidents of child labor are difficult to identify. A mine may put children in school on the day of inspection and then revert when the inspectors leave. First let’s identify a group that can witness these incidents. We know incidents like this happen because inspectors are not the only group of workers who visit mine sites. Often mining engineers, geologists or other workers will visit these sites and see incidents that they cannot act on. This is our identified group that is in a position to witness the incidents. Now, how can we make reporting easy for this group? Mining engineers and geologists are often well-paid and have access to technology. Perhaps a simple reporting app on a smart phone would suffice.

Next, how can we then incentivize them to make a report? Engineers and geologists have a code of ethics. Knowing that a report would make a difference might be enough to encourage them to participate. Perhaps we could take funds and give them to an NGO that works to end child labor with each report. On the other side they might be concerned for their safety as a whistleblower, so having a certain level of anonymity might also be important to encourage participation. Perhaps only the NGO would have access to the identity of who made the report.

Finally, how can we prevent false reports? We could try collecting some automated metadata such as date and time of a visit, or we could reduce the impact of individual reports so that a single report won’t be as damaging to a company. Perhaps the reports don’t automatically get assigned to shipments from the mine in question until the NGO is able to confirm them.

An implementation of the above may proceed as follows. We need to start with money. We identify a company sending miners and geologists to mine sites. These mine sites are all tracked on our token-based traceability system we outlined above. The company locks $20,000 in a smart contract. Once the money is locked in, the company’s engineers and geologists are able to use a simple reporting app to make a report whenever they visit a production site. Limiting reporting to people appointed by the company contributing the money is one way to reduce the incidence of false reports.

The report should be very simple, either stating the worker saw an incident of child labor, or that they did not. Either way, submitting the report transfers $10 from the smart contract to a non-profit tasked with following up on these incidents to clean up the supply chain.

The company locking in the money, as well as the engineers and geologists will be able to see an aggregation of how many reports have been made at that mine site, with anonymous data. After a certain number of incidents are reported, the NGO makes a surprise visit to the mine to verify the reports. If child labor is seen, the NGO adds a red flag on the blockchain to all shipments coming from the site.

With our above proposal you may have noticed a heavy use of uncertain words such as “perhaps” and “try.” Incident reporting is not a solved problem. Incidents happen because of complex dynamics with a lot of moving parts, including the individual motivations of workers, local culture and attitudes which may vary from region to region, economic stability in the region, and people in the position to report incidents being afraid of repercussions for doing so. Implementing a blockchain-powered system to address incident reporting may have a mix of positive outcomes and unintended consequences that are very difficult to predict and at this point we should expect solutions to be experimental.

### 9.9. CONCLUSION

With any industry disruption there will be winners and losers. The danger is that large established international mining companies and manufacturing brands win at the expense of poorer miners and local cottage industry. This does not have to be the case.

By designing the system with an equal playing field in mind, we have the potential to make it so that the winners are everyone who wants to participate in a responsible mineral supply chain, and the losers are armed groups sourcing using illicit practices.

We are seeing a significant shift in public awareness and governmental oversight of the mining supply chain, and with this shift has come the need for a safer, more transparent, accurate and detailed, reliable and cost-effective means to assure provenance. Blockchain technology can and will provide that solution.
