## 10

## TOKENIZATION OF THE JAPANESE REAL ESTATE MARKET

MIKE ROGERS

### 10.1. INTRODUCTION

The digital asset market is currently experiencing significant growth and increased interest in the area of asset tokenization. Going back to the days of Bitcoin’s “colored coins,” developers saw the opportunity in tracking ownership on a blockchain-based asset, thereby enabling secure asset transfers in a peer-to-peer form. Thenceforth, innovators have been creating protocols to modernize the methods by which asset creation, representation and ownership transfers occur online, most notably in the digital securities industry.

While the digital securities market continues to expand, advocacy groups around the globe are taking notice. Individuals, corporations and governments alike are exploring this powerful, feature rich method of financial technology (fintech). However, potential stakeholders primarily lack access to clear and reliable research demonstrating legitimate business use cases for tokenization.

This chapter illustrates the value proposition for utilizing digital securities in the Japanese real estate market. Through this unique financial mechanism, Japanese real estate managers can find solutions to current challenges, such as protracted investment fund duration, inefficient market pricing and underlying investment illiquidity: powered by distributed ledger technology (DLT), security token ecosystems are primed to unlock new capabilities within the ¥17.7 trillion (approx. US$160 billion) Japanese real estate market.

### 10.2. FINANCIAL TRANSFORMATION SPARKED BY BLOCKCHAIN

A blockchain is a distributed database structure that records transactions on a digital ledger, providing users with a secure and transparent history of events. In our ever increasing digital world, where countless amounts of data are vulnerable to alteration and theft, a blockchain’s composition provides enhanced security and assurance to its users.

As noted in previous chapters, blockchain security is built on the concepts of consensus and tamper-resistance. Consensus is achieved when network devices confirm both the reliability of transactions and the true state of a system. Once transactions are confirmed, the records of a blockchain are considered tamper-resistant, meaning they cannot be altered without an overriding consensus. Moreover, cryptography plays a key role in blockchain data security. Cryptographic hashing algorithms help protect the ledger’s transaction data by generating encryption keys to encrypt and decrypt pieces of information. Asymmetric cryptography, or public key cryptography (PKC), is used to secure digital asset wallets, ensuring that only the intended users have access.

In contrast with symmetric encryption algorithms, which use the same key to encrypt and decrypt data, PKC algorithms use one key for encryption (public key) and another key for decryption (private key). Digital asset wallet addresses are generated using public keys, allowing them to be safely shared with others. Private keys are used for digital signature creation and transaction verification; transactions are verified and added to the ledger after confirming the hash within the digital signature. Asymmetric cryptography systems are widely used throughout the digital assets industry to enhance transaction security, as asset transfers can only be authorized using the private key associated with its corresponding digital wallet.

When combining these features, the integrity of a blockchain’s data is upheld, offering a medium to transform financial products into cryptographically-secured digital tokens; these digital assets can be issued and transferred on DLT networks. DLT systems are modernizing financial services infrastructure and related processes, by creating shared data structures (ledgers) of electronic records that are authenticated through multiparty consensus mechanisms. As such, digital asset transactions are recorded in real time on an authoritative ledger that is readily available to appropriate network users. As discussed in detail in a later chapter, the DLT network model can eliminate or facilitate the reporting and auditing process to reduce costs and provide greater assurance. Financial instruments and related procedures are further transformed when integrating smart contracts into DLT systems.

### 10.3. SMART CONTRACT STANDARDS EXPEDITE DIGITAL ASSET FUNCTIONS

As mentioned in earlier chapters, smart contracts are protocols that digitally facilitate the performance of previously agreed upon terms and procedures. Transactions executed through smart contracts are conditional, as the underlying code follows “if, then” logic statements, meaning if a requirement is satisfied, then an action occurs. Smart contract activity is recorded on a distributed ledger, making all transactions trackable and tamper-resistant. The unique characteristics of smart contracts assure reliable transaction execution without third parties, resulting in reduced operating and transaction costs.

Smart contracts are conceptually similar to “stored procedures” used in traditional database structures when automating software. However, DLT smart contracts can automate business processes across distinct entities, while network rules offer guaranteed execution and outcomes are verifiable and auditable for all system participants. Likewise, the distributed computing platform “Ethereum” offers a turing complete scripting language, providing functionality for smart contract implementation using built-in standards.

Ethereum Request for Comments (ERC) standards describe the requisite set of functions for tokens, enabling smart contracts and related applications to communicate and execute functions in a predictable manner. The ERC20 standard provides a framework for the systemized creation of fungible tokens (ERC20 tokens), as well as balance tracking and transfer functional capabilities. By leveraging Ethereum’s robust platform, ERC20 tokens offer businesses a simple mechanism for creating their own digital assets. This novel functionality sparked a digital asset fundraising boom, which brought unconventional challenges to global financial regulation.

### 10.4. HARMONIZING FINANCIAL REGULATION ACROSS JURISDICTIONS

Major innovations in technology, such as the internet, coincide with a deeper integration of individual markets within the global financial system. In 1987, former SEC Chairman Charles Cox took note of this trend while testifying before Congress, stating

*as a result of a number of factors, including technological advances and the removal of restrictions on foreign participation by many of the world’s securities markets, internationalization is more than a developing trend, it is a present day reality.[¹](#CH010.html#fn1)*

The advancements in technology also bring about new challenges in cross-border regulation, as made evident by the recent innovations in blockchain and digital assets.

Regulation of businesses based in other jurisdictions requires local lawmakers to reflect on how their rules will apply to entities that would like to access their capital markets. In some instances, this simply requires both foreign and domestic companies to adhere to the same laws. However, regulators realize that this approach might not always be appropriate, leading to certain exemptions for foreign issuers and investors. This flexibility allows locales to reap the economic rewards of global enterprise, while at the same time protecting its citizens from potential frauds and abuses.

Between 2014 and mid-2018, over $11 billion was raised through digital assets. Mostly prompted by Ethereum’s ERC20 standard, this global phenomenon demonstrated the effectiveness of fundraising with DLT and smart contracts. However, most of the utility tokens created over this time period were issued through non-compliant initial coin offerings (ICOs). According to Satis Group, 78% of ICOs were identified scams, 7% failed or had gone dead, and only 15% went on to trade on an exchange.[²](#CH010.html#fn2) As such, innovators and policymakers recognized the apparent need for increased digital asset regulation.

On July 30, 2019, SEC Commissioner Hester Peirce spoke about the importance of cross-border cooperation in digital asset regulation at the Singapore University of Social Sciences Convergence Forum. She acknowledged that digital assets present unique concerns because

*countries all over the world are still in the early stages of determining how and whether to regulate crypto. Uncertainty about what the rules in any particular country are makes a determination of which country’s rules apply even more difficult.[³](#CH010.html#fn3)*

Absent a solitary worldwide regulatory framework, which is both unlikely and unwise, innovators need to plan for potential frictions between jurisdictions.

Digital asset regulatory frameworks need to be properly aligned with investor expectations, as the geographic locations of ecosystem participants often differ. Hester Peirce acknowledged this issue in Singapore, by stating

*If the investors, the platforms on which they are transacting, and the companies in which they are investing are all operating in one country, the investor knows which country’s rules apply, but things get more complicated when multiple jurisdictions are involved.*

Taking note of these challenges, digital securities entrepreneurs believe their products and services can offer a viable solution to the regulatory quagmire that is likely to accelerate as Web 3.0 approaches.

### 10.5. DIGITAL SECURITIES – MIGRATING TO WEB 3.0

The concept of a more trustworthy, equitable and open web was envisioned long ago, however, proper implementation required major advancements across computing disciplines over many decades. Luckily, recent breakthroughs in artificial intelligence, distributed systems and cloud computing, among other computing fields, have brought us closer to Web 3.0. The impending transformation of the way value is created, stored, transferred and redefined cannot be overstated. However, one must first understand the principles of the next web to truly grasp the coming paradigm shift.

The foundation of Web 3.0 is a “Semantic Web,” where internet data is linked together in a machine-readable format. According to the World Wide Web Consortium, a web of linked data would enable computers to do more useful work and to develop systems that can support trusted interactions over the network.[⁴](#CH010.html#fn4) As such, internet data would be sharable and reusable across application, venture and communal boundaries. Another tenet of Web 3.0 is the creation of a Stateful Internet or

*an internet that can handle data, and rules for changing the state of that data, in the way the internet handles messaging today – more or less decentralized – and that allows private stateful networks to interoperate with each other and the public network through standard protocols.[⁵](#CH010.html#fn5)*

Blockchain technology provides a medium for network participants to hold and transfer state over the internet, without the assistance of centralized service providers. A stateful internet and semantic web would give rise to a new digital economy, one that operates across multisided marketplaces online. DLT systems, smart contracts and security tokens will enable compliant interoperability between our current financial system and the digital value systems of tomorrow.

Security tokens or digital securities are designed to represent ownership in assets and/or entities. Digital securities offer the same advantages as conventional ERC20 tokens in terms of simplicity, efficiency and liquidity, but they also have the added benefit of programatically embedded regulatory compliance. By ensuring proper adherence to identity, jurisdiction, and asset category restrictions, digital securities have an opportunity to become a globally standardized mechanism by which regulated finance operates on Web 3.0.

Digital securities offer robust supervisory controls, making them an ideal vehicle for managing a multitude of regulatory jurisdictions. Governing laws and regulations are embedded in the protocol layer, providing adaptability and flexibility. A coded framework of preventive and detective controls can mitigate impacts of risk, leading to decreased compliance costs. As coded frameworks reduce compliance expenses, they will help streamline the facilitation of cross-border transactions between DLT systems. Smart contracts can certify strict adherence to all applicable regulations while executing any corporate action, such as voting, dividends, share-buy-backs and trading. The resulting decrease in governance-related frictions can allow for the creation of trustless businesses.

Through a combination of web platforms, DLT systems, smart contracts and blockchain-based assets, autonomous economic models and enterprise structures are finally made possible. Investors and market participants can find counterparties, discover prices, and trade digital instruments that represent ownership and economic rights. Organizational governance and decision-making can be formalized, automated and imposed using software.

By serving as a hybrid of investment instruments, agreements, contracts, and vehicles, digital securities can ensure the proper migration of today’s financial laws and regulations to the new digital economy. As such, security token usage is projected to grow significantly over the coming decades.

### 10.6. PROJECTED GROWTH OF SECURITY TOKENS

When classifying digital securities, we find it best to divide security tokens into two main categories, traditional security tokens and asset-backed security tokens (ABSTs). Traditional security tokens are digital assets issued during a security token offering (STO), a private funding method we examined in the Curzio Research chapter. These blockchain-based assets exist independently of the physical world, meaning they live and trade “on-chain.” By law, traditional security tokens must abide by the rules and regulations of each issuer, investor and subsequent holder.

As of 2019, most of the economic value in the digital securities industry has come from traditional security tokens used for startup financing. Chain Partners, a South Korean blockchain research and advisory firm, estimates that $5.1 billion of the current $102 billion total addressable startup fundraising market can be captured by security tokens in the future ([Fig. 10.1](#CH010.html#fig1)).

**Fig. 10.1:    Financing Through Security Token (STO).**

![image](images/C10-FIG1.jpg)

*Sources*: Chain Partners, lcodata; Crunchbase News; Thomson Reuters TAB data. *Note*: ICO figure is sum of Jan to Nov 2018.

Startup financing is the lifeblood for innovation brought about through early stage ventures, having served as one of the main catalysts for the initial rise of digital assets. From a fundraising standpoint, traditional security tokens have essentially become the regulatory compliant successor to utility tokens.

As per [Fig. 10.2](#CH010.html#fig2), Chain Partners projects the security token market will reach $2 trillion by 2030, expanding at a compound annual growth rate of 59% per year. However, the majority of this growth is expected to be fueled by the issuance of ABSTs.

**Fig. 10.2:    Expected Growth in Security Tokens.**

![image](images/C10-FIG2.jpg)

*Source*: Chain Partners. Used with permission.

ABSTs are digital assets created through the tokenization of pre-existing financial/real assets; the tokens are “on-chain” representations of assets that exist “off-ledger.” ABSTs are backed by and derive value from the underlying real-world assets, drawing conceptual parallels to asset-backed securities (ABS) from legacy finance.

ABSs are created through the powerful and innovative process of securitization. As defined by Columbia University Professor Dr Suresh Sundaresan, securitization is

*a framework in which some illiquid assets of a corporation or a financial institution are transformed into a package of securities backed by these assets, through careful packaging, credit enhancements, liquidity enhancements, and structuring. (Sundaresan, 1997)*

The flexibility of securitization has revolutionized the investable world, offering opportunities to both issuers and investors.

Technology has long been the enabler of new securitization methods; the advent of information technology allowed for the globalization of secured borrowing, catalyzing a multidecade boom for ABSs, as shown in [Fig. 10.3](#CH010.html#fig3).

**Fig. 10.3:    Growth in Asset-based Securities across Sectors.**

![image](images/C10-FIG3.jpg)

*Source*: Chain Partners. Used with permission.

In a similar fashion, blockchain technology has given rise to tokenized financial assets, an instrument that is particularly suitable for a global financial structure. In this case, by leveraging DLT’s advancements in communication, computation and recordkeeping, along with the automatic execution of smart contracts, ABSTs provide an ideal medium for digitizing real-world assets.

There is a symbiotic relationship between financial globalization and the ever increasing desire for transformative financial mechanisms, such as tokenization. Globalization calls for the conformity of parties across borders and specifically the ability of financiers to lay claim to the underlying assets of securities; tokenization is an evolution in market funding capabilities, allowing for the stateful existence and seamless transfer of digitally native securities over the internet.

Considering that ABSTs make up just 20% of the digital securities market to date, tokenized assets will grow tremendously over the coming decade. According to [Fig. 10.4](#CH010.html#fig4), Chain Partners projects the market supply of ABSTs to surpass that of traditional security tokens around the year 2021.

**Fig. 10.4:    Relative Projected Growth in ABSTs.**

![image](images/C10-FIG4.jpg)

*Source*: Chain Partners. Used with permission.

To attain these lofty expectations, institutionalization must first occur, requiring the creation of strong international standards, best practices and credible infrastructure. Luckily, the digital securities industry made significant strides in these areas throughout 2019 and continues to do so in 2020.

### 10.7. DIGITAL SECURITIES ECOSYSTEM

The security token ecosystem continues to expand, presently made up of primary issuance, trading and security platforms. Strategic resource and knowledge sharing adds significant value for all parties, as data-enhanced services and products are coming online.

The primary market mostly includes digital security platforms, such as Securitize. These providers facilitate the full lifecycle of digital securities ownership, beyond issuance, as well as the goals of each stakeholder: issuers, investors and exchanges. Securitize’s platform is powered by its DS Protocol (Digital Securities Protocol), a fluid and adaptive ownership architecture detailed extensively in the Curzio Research chapter. The DS Protocol creates a regulatory compliant internet ecosystem built atop a smart contract infrastructure of Digital Securities Services (DS Services) supporting third party Digital Securities Applications (DS Apps). All interactions between the security tokens and other smart contracts are standardized, enabling proper adherence to applicable securities laws and regulations throughout the lifecycle of each digital security.

The digital security secondary market is made up of multiple licensed Security Token Exchanges (STEs), among other marketplaces and liquidity providers. STEs are classified as alternative trading systems (ATS) under US regulations, and must be approved by the SEC. ATS, known as proprietary trading systems in Japan, are computer systems that match buyers and sellers to find counterparties for executing securities transactions. By disintermediating the services of traditional brokers and exchanges, ATS offer lower trading costs to investors seeking an alternative method for accessing liquidity.

Our Curzio Research chapter examined Open Finance Network, an ATS with a protocol for standardizing alternative assets, as well as the reporting, compliance, clearing and settlement processes related to those assets. The resulting interoperability creates a mechanism for tokenized trading to connect off-chain “traditional” assets and on-chain “digital” assets. Furthermore, market participants no longer need to rely on manually reconciled ledgers, making real-time verifications of asset ownership and funding availability possible. The instant settlement of tokenized assets will transform the typical securities transaction lifecycle.

Agents of the digital securities market have obtained the necessary licenses and regulatory approval to operate as investment service providers. Coupled with technological know-how, the bar has been raised for both quality and execution within the digital assets industry. Maturation has led traditional financial services firms to partner with the security token ecosystem, as seen through Securitize’s recent financing round that raised more than $14M from Santander InnoVentures, MUFG, Nomura Holdings and Sony Financial Ventures.[⁶](#CH010.html#fn6) Collaborations with trusted institutions like these further legitimizes digital securities, and should help accelerate security token adoption.

### 10.8. REAL ESTATE MARKET INEFFICIENCIES

The real estate industry is currently littered with inefficiencies; disparate distribution of reliable property data, ad hoc pricing, opaque ownership structures, fragmented information systems, analog (paper and other non-electronic) records, localized markets and siloed networks, among countless other deficiencies contribute to market transparency issues. Markets with lower levels of information transparency hinder investor confidence when making decisions, leading to adverse selection and inefficient pricing. Digital securities can provide a framework for modernizing the processes and procedures of an asset class notorious for its lack of real-time data.

Tokenized assets issued and transferred on DLT systems would increase data transparency and broaden market access, ultimately improving real estate liquidity and asset pricing efficiency. Through careful financial structuring techniques, ownership interests in real estate assets, as well as their underlying cash flows can be customized. Security tokens can be packaged together to represent a claim on cash flows from multiple properties, or they can represent fractional ownership in an individual property. Digital securities offer the flexibility of securitization, while also leveraging the open access of web-based platforms.

The flexibility of tokenization provides unique advantages to both issuers and investors. Protocol-based financial engineering allows tokens to be created from any type of cash flow, including those that would be economically inefficient under current structuring techniques. These tokens have a clearly defined mandate, serving as a financial intermediary between borrowers and investors. Issuers have a cost-effective method of leveraging assets on their balance sheets, thus providing access to lower-cost global funding. Capital providers can enjoy an array of options for investment deployment, diversifying portfolio risk. Moreover, tokens offering direct asset class exposure provide better risk calibration, helping investors form customized risk-return profiles.

The aforementioned benefits of security tokens can allow for a substantial increase in the liquidity of institutional real estate. As shown in [Fig. 10.5](#CH010.html#fig5), Resource Real Estate, LLC’s “The Illiquid-Liquid Spectrum,” institutional real estate is an extremely illiquid asset.

**Fig. 10.5:    Relative Liquidity of Assets.[⁷](#CH010.html#fn7)**

![image](images/C10-FIG5.jpg)

From a purchaser’s perspective, institutional real estate investments require large up-front costs, most of which are financed by local lenders. This limits the ability of lesser known purchasers to access lower-cost global capital markets for borrowing. Likewise, from the lender’s perspective, they are limited to regional markets which restrict their ability to transfer risk and seek out potentially higher performing asset classes for diversification. Regionally concentrated lenders can modulate loan exposure through DLT systems, offering pieces of these cash flows to a global pool of investors by way of security tokens. The subsequent token buyer would benefit from real estate exposure outside of their domicile, while the original lender can benefit from an alternative method of liquidity.

If executed properly, tokenization has the potential to increase the liquidity level of certain institutional real estate investments to that of “Traded REITs.” A movement of such magnitude along Resource Real Estate, LLC’s “Illiquid-Liquid Spectrum” would bring additional investors, increase market competitiveness and lower middlemen commissions, leading to more efficient asset pricing, higher profits for direct participants, and further reductions in institutional friction. The real estate investment trust (REIT) structure has made a profound impact on global real estate markets since its creation in 1960. Originating in the United States, REITs offered investors a vehicle to have exposure to large-scale, diverse and liquid portfolios of income-producing real estate assets. Sixty years after its introduction, countries around the world continue to pass laws adopting REITs for the first time, demonstrating current opportunities for unlocking value with creative structures in real estate markets around the world.

We believe that digital securities can provide an innovative framework for creating transformative real estate investment vehicles and instruments, leveraging technological advancements to track real estate market data. By providing real-time data to market participants, investors would have access to information needed to transact confidently. This would draw more actors to real estate markets, further increasing the amount of market data. The resulting feedback loop between buyers, sellers, industry actors and data points would lead to a more efficient price discovery process.

Digital securities are currently best suited for real estate markets with strong informational technology infrastructure, open data initiatives, and future-focused governments and businesses. Developed nations best meet these criteria, especially those that already have utilized the REIT structure for years, as digital securities would offer an innovative structuring to unlock further value. As such, we have come up with a multistep proposal for using DLT systems and asset tokenization to increase data transparency and investment liquidity within the Japanese real estate market.

### 10.9. THE JAPANESE REAL ESTATE MARKET

In this section we provide a brief overview of the Japanese real estate market. We first describe the economics and the size of the market. We then discuss some areas of deficiency and inefficiency in that market, which could be improved upon.

#### 10.9.1. Market Size and Investment Environment

Behind the United States and China but ahead of the UK, India and Germany, the Japanese economy is currently the third largest in the world, and is expected to hold this position well into the 2020s per nominal gross domestic product (GDP) forecasts by Nomura Research Institute, Ltd. (NRI) ([Fig. 10.6](#CH010.html#fig6)), making Japan an ideal target for capital deployment over the coming decade.

**Fig. 10.6:    GDP of Selected Countries.**

![image](images/C10-FIG6.jpg)

*Sources*: Compiled by NRI from IMF ‘World Economic Outlook Database (2017 Apr.)’ data

As reported by Sumitomo Mitsui Trust Research Institute (SMTRI), the market cap of real estate private funds that invested in Japanese domestic real estate totaled 17.7 trillion JPY (about 160 billion USD) in early 2019. When factoring in Japanese real estate investment trusts (J-REITs), which accounted for 17.9 trillion yen, the Japanese real estate market had nearly 36 trillion yen (about 340 billion USD) of assets under management. These results are illustrated in [Fig. 10.7](#CH010.html#fig7) found in SMTRI’s 27th “Survey on Private Real Estate Funds.” Note that while the portion of private funds has remained relatively steady over the years, the majority of growth in assets under management has been fueled by J-REITs.

When compared to other major real estate markets, Japan offers relatively high spreads to investors. According to Jones Lang LaSalle (JLL), prime office buildings in Tokyo had an average yield gap of 2.9% in 2019, which is higher than the average yield gap of both London (2.5%) and NYC (1.25%). As a result, foreign funds searching for yield have deployed a record amount of capital into Japanese real estate over the past several years: in the year 2017 alone, overseas firms spent 1.1 trillion yen on acquisitions. In fact, according to SMTRI’s survey, the relative attractiveness of Japan’s yield gap was the top reason for foreign investors choosing Japan ([Fig. 10.8](#CH010.html#fig8)).

**Fig. 10.7:    Funding Sources of Japanese Real Estate.**

![image](images/C10-FIG7.jpg)

Source: Data obtained from SMTRI Survey on Private Real Estate Funds in Japan January 2019 Results (p. 2)[⁸](#CH010.html#fn8).

**Fig. 10.8:    Factors That Attract Foreign Investors.**

![image](images/C10-FIG8.jpg)

***Source***: Data obtained from SMTRI Survey on Private Real Estate Funds in Japan January 2019 Results (p. 7)[⁹](#CH010.html#fn9).

Foreign investors, such as institutional real estate giants KKR and Blackstone, are also attracted to Japan’s ultra-low interest rate environment, which is set by the Bank of Japan. In an effort to promote growth, the Bank of Japan instituted policies that facilitate cheap financing markets, fueling real estate demand. Interestingly, some banks even pay businesses to borrow from them by issuing negative interest-bearing loans. These accommodative monetary policy conditions have prompted significant deployment of sovereign capital into Japanese real estate over the past decade, as displayed in [Fig. 10.9](#CH010.html#fig9), where the bottom bar shows the percentage of SMTRI survey respondents that managed capital in Japan from overseas.

#### 10.9.2. Japanese Real Estate Market Pain Points[^(10)](#CH010.html#fn10)

With the increasing likelihood of further easing of rates by the Bank of Japan, the competitive landscape for property acquisitions has intensified. Funds with an average investment period of 5+ years dominate this market, as the average length of new real estate funds launched in 2019 is now 9.1 years. The increased length in fund duration coincides with decreased liquidity, as investor capital is used to purchase and maintain the underlying real estate assets.

Japanese real estate securitization involves major players, such as GK-TK (GodoKaisha-TokumeiKumiai) and TMK (TokuteiMokutekiKaisha). Securitized products draw significant investment interests from global institutions looking to manage balance sheet risk and diversify cash flows. As institutional investors require higher yields than individuals, the recently inflated real estate prices make it difficult to form deals, particularly in metropolitan areas. In fact, when surveyed by SMTRI, nearly 25% of foreign investors chose “rising real estate prices” as a reason for not investing in the Japanese market ([Fig. 10.10](#CH010.html#fig10)).

**Fig. 10.9:    The Percentage of Japanese Real Estate Investment Funds with Overseas Investors.**

![image](images/C10-FIG9.jpg)

*Source*: Data obtained from SMTRI Survey on Private Real Estate Funds in Japan January 2019 Results (p. 2)

**Fig. 10.10:    Drawbacks in Japanese Real Estate Market That Deter Foreign Investment.[^(11)](#CH010.html#fn11)**

![image](images/C10-FIG10.jpg)

*Source*: Data obtained from SMTRI Survey on Private Real Estate Funds in Japan January 2019 Results (p. 7)[^(11)](#CH010.html#fn11).

When analyzing the business environment for J-REITs, the second most popular response to SMTRI’s survey found that managers were “seeking to develop a better understanding and gather information” before they launched open-ended private funds. These managers cited a significant need for an “expansion of the categories of investors to invest in funds” and “improvement in liquidity” as important factors for further development in the private REIT market ([Fig. 10.11](#CH010.html#fig11)).

**Fig. 10.11:    Factors That Would Enable Growth of Foreign Investment in Japanese Real Estate Market.**

![image](images/C10-FIG11.jpg)

*Source*: Data obtained from SMTRI Survey on Private Real Estate Funds in Japan January 2019 Results (p. 10)[^(12)](#CH010.html#fn12).

Hence, we believe that DLT systems and asset tokenization can provide solutions to the current challenges cited by fund managers that are inhibiting maximum investment in Japanese real estate.

### 10.10. TOKENIZING JAPANESE REAL ESTATE INVESTMENTS

Security tokens operating within DLT systems are designed to improve efficiencies of the price discovery process. Inefficiencies in the system currently restrict the Japanese real estate market; specifically those challenges that relate to investment data transparency, access and liquidity.

#### 10.10.1. Data Transparency

Real estate investment opportunities are often complex, requiring intensive due diligence by large teams of analysts and lawyers; significant up-front costs are necessary to properly evaluate prospects and assess the technicalities of related assets. These costs are often fixed in nature, resulting in a competitive advantage for those deploying capital at scale. As such, a threshold exists where large asset managers have a distinct edge over those with less capital.

Digital securities can help lower the initial capital deployment threshold, by leveraging DLT ecosystems and security token history to increase data transparency surrounding real estate assets. Enhancing data transparency would disseminate informational inputs that go into the investment decision-making process, encouraging investors with more modest resources to deploy capital. As a result, the global investor pool for Japanese real estate would expand, creating additional fund flow and consequently higher prices.

According to JLL’s “Global Real Estate Transparency Index 2018,” Japan ranked 19th out of the 100 countries surveyed; Japan rated toward the top of the “Transparent” group, but was still far from making the “Highly Transparent” tier ([Fig. 10.12](#CH010.html#fig12)). Since the last survey in 2016, 85% of countries saw an increase in real estate transparency, many of which outpaced Japan. The most notable increases were in those jurisdictions that relied on technology.

JLL’s survey revealed an uptrend in technology used to increase real estate data transparency. One example was seen in Dubai, where a building classification initiative leveraged an official government app for property broker and operator registration. The Netherlands was also recognized for utilizing real estate technology to encourage data sharing; the switch to open data made it easier to verify transaction prices and other registered information.

**Fig. 10.12:    Transparency Scores of Selected Countries.[^(13)](#CH010.html#fn13)**

![image](images/C10-FIG12.jpg)

JLL’s 2018 report highlighted the recent rise of 250+ proptech (property technology) startups, which collectively raised over $6 billion in funding since its 2016 Transparency Index report. These tech-enabled platforms are generating significant amounts of fresh data and creating new metrics, fueling investor and business expectations for a significant leap in real estate transparency. However, the industry is failing to keep pace with the higher anticipated standards, as there are challenges around data access, quality and consistency. We believe that the security token ecosystem can assist with these areas of concern, particularly within the Japanese real estate market.

JLL researcher, Yuto Ohigashi commented on Japan’s need to act, stating:

*transparency in the Japanese market has improved, but other counties are achieving even more significant improvement. Japan will be left behind the global trend unless it increases the speed of improvement.[^(14)](#CH010.html#fn14)*

As noted earlier, many Japanese real estate fund managers are seeking to develop a better understanding and gather information before they allocate more capital to the market. This statement coincides with Japan ranking 35th in “Transaction Process” on JLL’s survey, which was mostly attributed to lack of transparency and validity of transaction price information. Japan’s embracement of blockchain technology is an effort to enhance transparency of real estate prices. Additionally, its government supports open data initiatives making it an ideal sandbox for tokenization solutions.

One proposal for increasing real estate transparency focuses on creating a real-time transparency index, employing a network of DLT systems and security tokens to aggregate and share Japanese market data among users. With the JLL Global Real Estate Transparency Index (GRETI) serving as a framework, we believe that technology is at the point where most of JLL’s 186 individual transparency measures can be compiled and displayed on a continuous basis; this would be a significant upgrade from the current methodology, where the index is updated every two years. The quantitative data can be collected from a combination of (1) proptech platform APIs (application programing interfaces); (2) real estate blockchain technology application initiatives, such as land title and deed records, property sale and title assignment, and investor/tenant identity among others; and (3) the underlying data collected throughout each security tokens lifecycle.

The qualitative factors can be aggregated from a consortium of industry experts using the DLT network; these practitioners would add granularity for actionable data, leveraging insights from their respective area of competence. The timeline for qualitative inputs would be decided by the participating members through consensus (IE: Daily, Weekly, Quarterly, etc.). Our proposal would satisfy the growing demand from investors and businesses for higher transparency standards; technology is raising aspirations for quality information to be delivered in a timely manner. However, a wide gap exists between expectations and reality, as made evident throughout JLL’s 2018 GRETI report.

Users of our proposed network would be incentivized to contribute data in exchange for access to real-time real estate transparency information, as opposed to waiting two years for JLL updates. The JLL GRETI currently serves as an industry benchmark for real estate fund managers and other decision-makers. As per Joseph L. Pagliari, Clinical Professor of Real Estate, Booth School of Business, University of Chicago:

*The Global Real Estate Transparency Index has long been the gold standard by which many in the international real estate investment community have attempted to assess and price risk.[^(15)](#CH010.html#fn15)*

A real-time transparency index would prove invaluable to international investment decision-makers, such as those looking to deploy capital into the Japanese real estate market from abroad.

If digital securities serve as the vehicle for intermediation between transacting parties, our proposed network of DLT systems can provide further visibility into data related to underlying Japanese properties. Upon initial issuance, property information, such as land title, capitalization and occupancy rates, ownership and tenant history, etc., can be tracked in real time throughout the lifecycle of each digital security. This information is typically scattered across multiple organizations and presented in differing formats; the data can now be standardized and made readily available on web-based platforms using DLT systems.

We envision network users initially being those that are not in the top tier of global real estate investment management, particularly those real estate managers that struggle to get above the initial capital deployment threshold. The main motivation for network participants would be access to crowdsourced real estate market information that is more valuable in the aggregate, than the individual pieces each actor would accumulate on their own. One might consider this network to be the next web’s version of a business ecosystem, which Harvard Professor James F. Moore (1996) defined as:

*an economic community supported by a foundation of interacting organizations and individuals – the organisms of the business world. The economic community produces goods and services of value to customers, who are themselves members of the ecosystem. The member organisms also include suppliers, lead producers, competitors, and other stakeholders. Over time, they coevolve their capabilities and roles, and tend to align themselves with the directions set by one or more central companies. Those companies holding leadership roles may change over time, but the function of ecosystem leader is valued by the community because it enables members to move toward shared visions to align their investments, and to find mutually supportive roles.*

The value of our proposed ecosystem would rise exponentially as Web 3.0 technologies are further integrated into business processes and everyday society, creating an unparalleled trove of real estate market data.

Institutional real estate firms would eventually be incentivized to join this network, once the business environment radically evolves. As per Tom Gruber, Co-founder and CTO of Intraspect Software, “Globalization is eliminating the traditional advantages of the large corporation: access to capital, access to markets, and economies of scale.”[^(16)](#CH010.html#fn16) In a similar manner, we believe that DLT ecosystems, smart contracts, security tokens and other Web 3.0 technologies can transform the methods and incentives surrounding data transparency in the Japanese real estate market today, and eventually the global real estate industry of tomorrow.

Yuto Ohigashi of JLL believes “there are two keys to increasing transparency: changing the market mechanism through government initiatives, and having private market players be conscious of transparency in their business activities.”[^(17)](#CH010.html#fn17) Japanese open data initiatives are incentivizing the creation of DLT systems that can serve as a standardized network for aggregating and sharing real estate data, while digital securities will soon track Japanese real estate market transactions, which we believe would generate access to further international investment.

#### 10.10.2. Open Access

JLL’s 2018 survey found that real estate investors gravitated toward markets that were open and transparent. Their research found that “Highly Transparent” cities account for 70% of direct real estate investment across the 158 cities covered, and nearly three-quarters of cross-border investment. Investors and property developers are increasingly requiring more information related to city-level dynamics. As made evident by [Fig. 10.13](#CH010.html#fig13), a meaningful correlation was found between a cities’ perceived real estate transparency and its total transaction volume.

Tokyo had a transparency score of 1.98, ranking at 50th among global cities. Even though Japan’s largest metropolitan area fell within the second transparency tier, it was still impressively ranked a top 3 global city by real estate transaction volume. In order to see the necessary uptick in real estate transaction volume, Japanese real estate managers will likely need to capture a larger share of the international investment community. According to Savills World Research, there was a sharp decrease in the percentage of cross-border investment volumes between 2017 and the third quarter of 2018 ([Fig. 10.14](#CH010.html#fig14)).

**Fig. 10.13:    Real Estate Transparency Index and Investment Volumes.[^(18)](#CH010.html#fn18)**

![image](images/C10-FIG13.jpg)

**Fig. 10.14:    Investment Volumes and Overseas Investment.[^(19)](#CH010.html#fn19)**

![image](images/C10-FIG14.jpg)

In December 2018, Japan saw a year-over-year decrease from international investments amounting to over 2 trillion yen, which was the largest percentage decrease since the 2008 Financial Crisis. This data validates SMTRI’s survey results, where real estate investment managers sought an “expansion of the categories of investors to invest in funds.” The tokenization of Japanese real estate can allow for the creation of diverse products that would attract alternative investors worldwide.

As discussed earlier, security tokens are an ideal vehicle for facilitating seamless cross-border transactions, due to their programmable nature. Tokenized Japanese real estate can provide a coded framework for preventive and detective asset controls, which would help mitigate jurisdictional regulatory concerns. According to JLL’s 2018 Index, Japan ranked 31st in “Governance in Listed Vehicles,” which was a contributing factor to the decrease in international investment. If digital securities became a preferred vehicle for listings, governance could be standardized and automated. International investors would have a more convenient mechanism for participating in governance actions, leading to a corresponding increase in the deployment of cross-border capital; a globally diverse investor pool would enhance market liquidity.

#### 10.10.3. Enhanced Liquidity

As noted in the previous section, nearly half of Japan’s real estate Assets Under Management are locked up in the private market, resulting in a large liquidity premium. The liquidity premium is primarily due to high transaction costs, which are composed of a wide range of market frictions: large bid-ask spreads, decreased market depth, high property search costs and brokerage fees, all of which increase market complexity.

Security tokens can increase liquidity within Japan’s private real estate market by enhancing characteristics of the underlying investments. The current model is heavily reliant on intermediaries, such as real estate bankers and brokers, and the buyer base for these complex assets is limited. By providing an asset standardization framework, digital securities can reduce analysis and search costs. When supply and demand frictions are smoothed, transactions can occur more frequently, leading to increased price transparency. As shown in [Fig. 10.15](#CH010.html#fig15), the resulting feedback loop would ultimately lead to higher liquidity for private real estate investments.

The tokenization of real estate can also add value to the supply side of the Japanese real estate market. As noted in the prior section, rising real estate prices are one of the top reasons why fund managers would overlook Japan. The rapidly inflating real estate prices can mostly be attributed to a limited supply of investment-grade real estate. Through tokenization, real estate assets can be pooled together and repackaged into interest-bearing digital securities. Diversification allows default risk to be spread, leading to an increase in the supply of investment grade real estate opportunities.

**Fig. 10.15:    Reasons for Illiquidity in Private Markets.[^(20)](#CH010.html#fn20)**

![image](images/C10-FIG15.jpg)

The tokenization of private real estate funds would be another application that would increase investment liquidity within the Japanese real estate market. As noted earlier, private Japanese real estate funds have an average investment period of 5+ years, while recently launched funds have an estimated lifespan of 9.1 years. These extensive fund periods keep capital locked up for an extended duration, which deters some investors from contributing capital to private real estate funds. This is especially discouraging, as private real estate funds often have opportunities to invest in high quality properties, driving outsized returns. Another downside of private investment funds is the high potential for a J-Curve effect ([Fig. 10.16](#CH010.html#fig16)), where fees are charged and returns are slow to come in as committed capital is not drawn down in the early years of the fund, due to a lack of viable investment opportunities.

**Fig. 10.16:    The J-curve of Investment Returns.**

![image](images/C10-FIG16.jpg)

By increasing real estate transparency, opening access to the international investment community, and providing greater liquidity, digital securities can ultimately contribute to maximizing price efficiency within the Japanese real estate market. As such, we believe that tokenization would drive further capital deployment to Japan from overseas investors. Furthermore, the Japanese real estate industry would be well ahead of its international counterparts when it comes to preparing for the new digital economy of Web 3.0.

### 10.11. CONCLUSION

Leaders throughout financial services believe that blockchain technology can transform the global financial system, serving as an underpinning to the financial infrastructure of the new digital economy. By increasing data efficiency, transparency and security, DLT can modernize or even replace processes typically performed by intermediaries. When coupled with smart contracts, DLT can bring about the next great evolution in financial product creation with asset tokenization.

Digital securities allow for the standardized issuance and ongoing transfer of tokenized assets on DLT systems. Similar to other novel financial transformation mechanisms of the past, tokenization will change the structure of once illiquid and uneconomical assets, making them available to a broader pool of global investors. Other unique innovations, such as built-in regulatory controls, will eliminate the need for wasteful compliance costs, further expanding the universe of assets that can be issued and traded.

We began this chapter with the goal of exploring the potential impact that tokenization might have on the Japanese real estate market. Much of the allure of DLT systems involves uniting parties around the world in common enterprise, further incentivizing information sharing to facilitate cross-border transactions. Digital securities offer a framework for achieving this goal, while also adhering to time-tested laws and regulations of the current world order. This study has outlined the benefits that asset tokenization can offer not just to Japanese real estate fund managers, but broadly to the global financial community, as society and markets prepare for the coming era of digitized finance.
