## 11

## BLOCKCHAIN-SUPPORTED BUSINESS INNOVATIONS

### 11.1. INTRODUCTION

The previous chapters discussed, in detail, some interesting use cases of blockchain. In this chapter, we briefly discuss some innovative applications and business models that have emerged. As cryptoassets became mainstream and much capital started flowing, the tax authorities took notice. Hence, there were tax regulations forthcoming which have created opportunities for tax service firms to educate and serve taxpayers. We discuss these developments in the next section.

Other ancillary services in finance and accounting have come to being to support the development of cryptocurrencies. One such application is discussed in Section 11.3. A new business model has emerged to grant loans in real currency while holding cryptocurrency as collateral. This simple transaction requires support services to ensure that collateral remains safe and secure.

Applications of blockchain for recordkeeping in the automobile industry are popular. In the automotive industry, particularly in the market of used cars, there usually is information asymmetry between the buyer and the seller. This leads to a loss of trust and hence a high transaction cost and uncertainty. Blockchain applications can provide immutable and comprehensive records, generating trust in a “trustless” environment. This and other applications in automotive industry are discussed in Section 11.4.

In the following section, Section 11.5, a conceptual case of peer-to-peer parking space rental is discussed. The model is similar to Airbnb but for daily parking spaces. In the current economy, certain individuals possess resources of value without knowing how to monetize those. This is due to friction in the system. Blockchain solution may make it possible for peer-to-peer transactions possible.

A unique yet simple application of blockchain in insurance industry is discussed in Section 11.6. With the aid of blockchain, underwriting of insurance and settlement of claims can be self-executed through smart contracts. Quick and automatic resolution of claim leads to efficiency and increased customer satisfaction. Moreover, reduced operating costs open up new marginal markets for insurance providers.

In Section 11.7, we discuss the concept of provenance and how features of blockchain technology are well suited to establish provenance. The issue of provenance is becoming important in the age of social consciousness and consumers choosing sustainable and ethical purchase options. The issue of provenance is particularly relevant in the agricultural industry. By reducing transaction costs, and thereby facilitating microtransactions, blockchain technology is using its potential to adapt popular and useful financial products to people with lesser means. The chapter concludes in Section 11.8.

### 11.2. CRYPTOTAX BUSINESS MODEL

As the popularity of cryptoassets and its widespread use increases worldwide, regulators and taxing bodies all over the world are developing and issuing regulatory guidelines on taxation laws. 2019 was a milestone year for cryptotaxation, as regulators around the world adjusted their tax policies and issued updated guidance on the tax treatment of cryptoassets. The complexity and variation in these guidelines are creating new business opportunities for accountants and tax experts. While the United States and the United Kingdom recently issued more thorough guidelines in late 2019, countries like South Korea are in the process of developing the regulations and intend to release those in 2020.

In 2014, the Internal Revenue Services (IRS) in the United States was one of the first regulatory bodies to publish a comprehensive guidance on tax treatment of cryptocurrencies.[¹](#CH011.html#fn1) Early on, the IRS recognized the dual purpose of cryptocurrencies – to pay for goods or to be held as investment. Though these types of currencies operate as “real money” they do not have legal tender status in any jurisdiction. Hence for tax purposes, cryptocurrency was treated as property and generally the tax laws governing the acquisition and disposal of property were applicable to cryptocurrencies. The 2019 tax form for individuals in the United States (Form 1040) includes a direct question on cryptoassets (see [Fig. 11.1](#CH011.html#fig1)). New IRS guidance issued in October 2019, includes tax treatment on “hard forks” and “airdrops.” They illustrate the complexity of the treatment through hypothetical examples.

**Fig. 11.1:    Schedule 1 of the Individual Tax Form in the United States.**

![image](images/C11-FIG1.jpg)

In early 2020 a blockchain tech firm, Lukka, unveiled a “do-it-yourself” tax tool to help individuals and businesses calculate their tax obligation to the IRS. The tool, developed in collaboration with [CPA.com](http://CPA.com), was released ahead of the tax filing season and in the wake of the recent IRS guideline to help taxpayers better understand the underlying tax laws on cryptoinvestments.

Likewise, in December 2019, the UK also released its cryptotax guideline[²](#CH011.html#fn2) on cryptoexchanges and cryptomining activities among others. The guideline maintains that cryptocurrencies, like Bitcoin, will not be considered money but will be categorized as commodity, but leaves open the possibility of reconsideration as the industry evolves, noting:

*The cryptoassets sector is fast-moving and developing all the time. The terminology, types of coins, tokens and transactions can vary. The tax treatment of cryptoassets continues to develop due to the evolving nature of the underlying technology and the areas in which cryptoassets are used. As such, HMRC will look at the facts of each case and apply the relevant tax provisions according to what has actually taken place (rather than by reference to terminology).[³](#CH011.html#fn3)*

There are many types of tax nuances based on whether the crypto was mined or acquired in an exchange. Additionally, while sometimes these are taxed as income, there are situations when only capital gains are taxed. Additionally, the taxable value of the cryptocurrency varies depending on the situation. The various activities of obtaining cryptoassets are identified as:

*   Buying and selling of tokens.
    
*   Exchanging tokens for other assets.
    
*   Mining.
    
*   Providing goods or services in return for cryptoassets.
    

Additionally, the tax treatment of these activities depends on the party involved and the purpose of the transaction, and is likely to incur one or more of the following taxes:

*   Capital gains tax.
    
*   Corporation tax.
    
*   Income tax.
    
*   National insurance contributions.
    
*   Stamp taxes.
    
*   Value Added Tax, or VAT.
    

The complexities and various possibilities with significant economic consequences for tax payers make ancillary services of cryptotax profitable and growing. Additionally, the variation in taxing regulations across jurisdictions would lead to tax advisory services for better tax management for high net worth individuals by exploiting these differences. Moreover, corporations might use these vehicles to create “virtual” tax havens. Thus, the role of accountants and tax experts are likely to grow, and not diminish, as a consequence.

### 11.3. COLLATERALIZED LENDING IN CRYPTOWORLD

Throughout the ages, people all over the world, have occasionally found themselves with a common problem – need for fast cash. A common solution to this problem emerged from ancient China to ancient Greece – collateralized borrowing. Collateralized borrowing, or pawning as it is commonly known, involves temporary exchange of any non-monetary asset for cash which is expected to be reversed within a specified time. That is, the borrower surrenders something of value to the lender for cash, later the borrower regains possession of the item once the loan is repaid. In instances when the borrower is unable to pay back their loan, the lender obtains ownership of the collateral and can sell the collateral and recover the loan amount plus a nominal profit. This mechanism of obtaining short-term cash, without necessarily disposing off assets, is not just for the poor. Famously, in the fourteenth century King Edward II of England pawned royal family jewels to help finance the war against France, and Queen Isabella of Spain is said to have borrowed against her jewelry to fund Christopher Columbus’s expedition to the New World.

The concept of collateralized borrowing has been adapted for contemporary needs and modern technology. It now impacts most adults in the U.S. Mortgage and auto loans are two of the most common form of collateralized borrowing. At the outset, the lender remits cash that is used toward the purchase of the house or the car. Subsequently, the borrower makes periodic payments, exceeding the interest amount, toward paying off the loan. The borrower, in the meanwhile, gains the right of use of the asset. If the borrower sells the asset, the lender has the first right toward the proceeds from the sale to satisfy any outstanding loan balance. Often, the lender holds the title to the property until the loan is paid in full, thereby preventing a sale of the collateralized asset without their knowledge. In instances when the borrower becomes delinquent or is unable to pay the loan, the lender seizes the asset and forecloses on the loan. The lender then sells off the asset to recover the unpaid balance.

Brokerage firms have adapted collaterailized financing in the investing domain by allowing investment on margin. Investment on margin allows an investor to invest an amount greater than their liquid assets through obtaining a loan using securities in their accounts as collateral. For example, if an individual has $10,000 to invest in the market, she buys securities worth $10,000 and uses those securities as collateral to borrow an additional amount, say $8,000, which she also invests. Thus, with a seed capital of $10,000 the individual can purchase $18,000 of securities. Now assume her investment gains 5% over a span of time when the loan has accrued 1% interest. In her original investment of $10,000 the investor would have made a modest $500 (or 5% on their investment), but through margin she nets $820 ($900 of return less $80 in interest), or 8.2% on their investment of $10,000. If her investment increases in value at a rate exceeding the interest on the loan, she earns a higher rate of return than the underlying market. Leverage makes a good deal even better. However, when market declines and the investment loses value, she suffers larger losses since she still has to make the interest payment. Moreover, when the investment loses value the lender may, as a preventive measure to secure their loan value, sell some of the investment to reduce the amount of loan outstanding.

Businesses also use collateral in their financing activities. Two traditional methods are through factoring of accounts receivables and pledging of inventory. Most businesses sell on credit to the customers, generating revenue but not cash at the time of sale. Cash is subsequently collected when the money is received from the customer. In the meantime, the company has accounts receivables, which is a promise of future cash inflow. A fast-growing business often faces cash flow problems due to the delay in collecting on the accounts receivables. A business in need of immediate cash could approach a lender and borrow cash, selling its receivables at a discount to compensate the lender for interest charges as well as the risk of non-payment. The lender then collects on the invoices, and remits the excess to the business after the principal balance and accrued interest on the loan is fully paid off. The benefit of factoring is that the business obtains the use of cash without applying or qualifying for a loan, thereby bypassing traditional banking channels. Likewise, inventory can be pledged as collateral to obtain a commercial loan. If the borrower defaults on the loan conditions, the lender may liquidate the inventory to meet the terms of the loan. The lender may also regularly and periodically inspect the pledged inventory to ensure that the pledged inventory is not obsolete or otherwise diminished in value.

Collateralized loan is popular because it provides benefits to both the lender and the borrower. The pledged assets, or collateralized assets, gives lenders a level of reassurance should the borrower default. This mitigates the risk for the lender, enabling the lender to charge a lower interest rate than they otherwise would. For the borrowers, they get the use of cash without having to liquidate their asset, thereby maintaining the right to use the asset as well as benefit from the potential appreciation of value of the asset. Additionally, it allows the borrower to avert the tax consequences that may arise from the sale of the asset, such as paying taxes on capital gains.

The lender takes on the risk of not recovering the principal and interest on the loan, should the borrower default on payments. Collateralized loan helps mitigate this risk, by providing the value of underlying assets as a form of guarantee toward future payments. However, some risks remain and are based on the quality of the collateral. These are:

*   As the collateralized asset acts as a guarantee toward the loan, a drop in value of the asset poses risk for the lender. For example, say a lender grants a $100 loan based on a collateralized asset with market value of $100. Suppose the asset, pledged as collateral, declines in value to $90, the borrower may simply forgo the asset and not repay the loan, with the lender absorbing a $10 loss. To prevent against such occurrences, lenders typically require collateral of greater value than the loan amount. This is known as “Loan to value ratio,” which typically is around 0.8. In other words, the collateralized asset is 125% (1/0.8) of the loan amount. Having a lower loan to value ratio provides greater protection to the lender. The higher the volatility of the asset value, the lower the loan to value ratio is expected to be.
    
*   The lender wants to prevent the borrower from selling, or otherwise disposing off, the collateralized asset without the lender’s knowledge or consent. In traditional pawnshops, the lender typically takes physical custody of the asset, prohibiting the sale of the asset. In mortgages and auto loans, the lender retains the title to the assets, thereby precluding sale of the asset without their knowledge.
    
*   Lenders typically prevent the borrower from using the same asset as collateral toward multiple loans. Technically, that would yield a greater loan to value ratio than is acceptable to any of the lenders. Moreover, in case of default by the borrower, the sale of collateralized asset may not be sufficient to cover all the loan balances, resulting in losses for some or all the lenders.
    

New-age businesses have been started that use digital assets as collaterals for loans denominated in US $s. This is beneficial for individuals who have most of their personal wealth invested in digital currency. It is also applicable to international small and medium enterprises (SMEs) who may not be able to get traditional loans in US $s, since large banks mostly deal with larger foreign corporations.

The cryptolending organization grants short-term cash loans, denominated in US $s, while holding on to many forms of cryptocurrency (such as Bitcoin and Litecoin) as security toward repayment. Typically, the interest rate is about 8%. Also, to safeguard against the volatility of cryptoassets, typical loan to value ratio is about 35–50%. Moreover, when the loan to value ratio exceeds 70%, the borrower is required to transfer additional collateral, or payoff part of the loan to maintain the loan to value ratio under 70%.

At the origination of the loan, the lender requires the borrower to transfer digital assets to a secured wallet maintained with a third party. The custodian of the collateralized asset generates a unique wallet address for each borrower. The borrowers transfer their cryptoasset to their respective wallet through the blockchain. The borrowers have to be assured that the digital assets they are transferring will be secured and returned to them once the loan is paid off. The third-party securer of the digitized asset, would move the digitized asset offline into a vaulted storage system, known as “Cold Storage.” Digitized assets stored in a “hot wallet” are available for transactions, however, those stored in “cold storage” are not. As an independent custodian of digital assets, such organizations have to maintain sophisticated internal control system to ensure safe keeping of the digital assets in their custody. They do so primarily through authorization and segregation of duties. Access to “cold storage” would require multifactor authentication and “tiered access controls” restrict access to employees. Collateral for each loan is stored separately, in different “cold storage,” access to each protected by a unique private key. Moreover, the hardware is geographically dispersed as a precaution against physical threat, following best practices of disaster recovery protocol.

After the loan is initiated and the collateral transferred to the “cold storage,” the lender continues to monitor real-time value of the collateral. An automated alert, or a margin call, is sent to the borrower should the loan to value ratio were to exceed 0.7. On the receipt of the margin call, the borrower is asked to transfer additional digital assets to their cold storage wallet to restore the loan to value ratio. Upon receipt of a margin call, the borrower could potentially pay off all or part of the loan. In instances when the borrower does not respond to the margin call, the lender liquidates the collateral to fulfill the borrower’s obligation and transfers the remaining collateral back to the borrower.

In early 2018, when the value of digital currency was rapidly declining, risk management protocols for the lenders were triggered. As the loan to value ratio exceeded 0.7, email warnings were dispatched. The borrowers were notified of the impending margin call and asked to transfer additional collateral or payoff the loan. In the experience of one such lending company, most customers chose to add additional collateral to rebalance their loan to value ratio. However, some customers, anxious of the market turbulence, chose to pay off their loan early.

The rapid development of collateralizing cryptoassets has been facilitated through the blockchain technology. Digitized assets can easily be transferred to the lender and the entire loan transaction could occur in absence of a physical meeting between the borrower and the lender. This allows the lender to efficiently reach markets which otherwise would be prohibitively costly. Furthermore, it allows the cryptoborrowers the flexibility to locate the cheapest source of capital. The transfer, storage, regular monitoring and eventual return of the collateral is automated and monitored algorithmically. The internal controls around the process are adaptations of traditional controls, such as authorization, authentication and segregation of duties, in a digitized environment.

Traditional risks of collateralized lending are mitigated in cryptolending. As discussed earlier, one of the systemic risks is for lenders to ensure that the collateralized asset is secured and the borrower cannot sell or use it as a collateral toward another loan. The blockchain, by design, does not allow people to “double-spend” or pledge the same asset twice. Once they sent their collateral to the “secured wallet” of the lender, they no longer have access to those assets, until the loan is paid off. The other prevalent risk that lenders bear is that of sudden and permanent decline in value of the collateral. Real-time algorithmic monitoring of the collateral value and the requirement of a conservative loan to value ratio limits the lender’s exposure to volatile markets. Moreover, geographical location and physical monitoring of the collateral is no longer a concern, allowing individuals and businesses all over the world access to loans in US $s, without having to go through elaborate and time-consuming process of applying for loans at institutional banks.

### 11.4. APPLICATION IN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY

The used car market is rife with “lemons” and the mantra always has been “buyer beware.” An unsophisticated buyer often doesn’t know the condition of the car, whether it has been in accidents, whether regular maintenance were undertaken on a regular basis, have there been major failures or repairs, etc. The used car market is rife with uncertainties and no one really trusts the used car salesman. This inherent lack of trust between the buyer and seller, typical in this market, presents an opportunity for blockchain-based solution.

For an automobile, there are many parties involved through its lifecycle: the original manufacturer, the original dealer, mechanics, insurance agents, previous owners, Division of Motor Vehicles, etc. Moreover, there are easy opportunities for manipulation and forgery. The seller could easily manipulate the speedometer, or lie about regular maintenance, or major forthcoming repairs. A blockchain solution is proposed, connecting various stakeholders into a distributed ledger system as shown in [Fig. 11.2](#CH011.html#fig2).

An application called “Car Dossier”[⁴](#CH011.html#fn4) provides a digital dossier based on blockchain that stores all the relevant information about the car from its manufacturing onwards. It includes ownership details, records of accidents, major repairs, regular maintenance, insurance coverage, registration documents, etc., by connecting different parties into a unified system.

Another interesting blockchain solution being developed by Ernst and Young (EY) is to distribute ownership of cars to multiple owners. With the rising costs of owning, maintaining and parking the car, millennials living in big cities usually forgo owning a car. Typically a car is used only for a fraction of the day, creating idle capacity. EY is creating a blockchain-based mobility platform named Tesseract.[⁵](#CH011.html#fn5) The platform facilitates recordkeeping for multiple ownership of the car and distributes the cost of ownership in a transparent and verifiable manner. In the Tesseract platform, the vehicles and trips are digitally logged on the blockchain. Transactions are automatically settled via smart contracts between owners, operators and third-party service providers. The ownership of the car can be flexible and fractional across multiple owners.

**Fig. 11.2:    A Blockchain of Distributed Recordkeeping of an Automobile’s History.**

![image](images/C11-FIG2.jpg)

The Tesseract enables peer-to-peer transactions between the owner of the car and the leaser. Such microtransactions are not occurring currently due to the friction caused by transaction costs and the recordkeeping costs. Once these costs are minimized and trust is built into the system through the aid of technology, such transactions become increasingly feasible. The blockchain enabled systems also harness the key advantages of the technology in terms of maintaining immutable and complete records of ownership and usage.

Similarly Deloitte has developed few blockchain-based solutions for automotive industry.[⁶](#CH011.html#fn6) It has created a customer loyalty platform, wherein all the consumer purchases can be maintained. It would facilitate instant redemption of these points avoiding delays and inconveniences. It has another application that stores and updates vehicle data on ownership, mileage and maintenance history from various sources. Similar, to the EY example above, Deloitte has also developed an application that will facilitate peer-to-peer car sharing by ensuring safe and secure payments.

### 11.5. MICRORENTAL OF PARKING SPACES

Similar to car sharing application discussed above, and the Airbnb type of business model of peer-to-peer transactions, one potential untapped business opportunity is to facilitate sharing of parking spaces. At the present time, there are no developments in this area, hence the discussion is conceptual. The concept utilizes the blockchain framework of facilitating microtransaction by creating trust between unknown parties through minimal or modest transaction fees.

Consider the example of an individual living in any of the metropolitan cities. Suppose they reside in the downtown or central business district area, but work in the suburbs. In their residence, apartment or house, say they have two parking spaces. Since the homeowners work in the suburbs and conceivably drive to work, their parking spots are vacant on weekdays during regular business hours. Typically, parking is expensive in the central business districts of major metropolitan areas, and typically charge $5 or so per hour, capping out at about $25 for the day.

The homeowner in our example possesses a valuable asset, two parking spots in central business district, however they are an underutilized resource. These two parking spaces have the potential of earning about $50 a day or about $10,000 a year. What prohibits the resident from renting out the parking space and earn about $10,000 per year is the friction in the system and the transaction costs. How does the homeowner find commuters wanting to park, how does the homeowner monitors the time and collect the payment, and overcome many other such logistical issues. Unable to find feasible solution to these issues, the parking space remains empty and the homeowner deprived of easy income. If a platform was to become available that would ease many of those concerns or problems, more personal parking spaces in these congested neighborhoods would become available for commercial use, becoming a source of revenue for homeowners while simultaneously reducing the cost of parking, and thereby benefitting the commuter.

The blockchain infrastructure has the potential of facilitating this exchange between the owner of private parking and the commuter on a daily basis. Sensors and internet of things could help with setting up the monitoring system and a smart contract mechanism would facilitate transfer of funds, thereby bringing trust into the system. Many other similar microtransaction possibilities are conceivable once the transaction fees to generate trust, for these transactions, are substantially reduced.

### 11.6. INSURANCE TO PROTECT AGAINST FLIGHT DELAYS AND CANCELLATION

An interesting and innovative new business model was trialed by the insurance industry. Typically, insurance industry is archaic and not known to be quick adopter of emerging technology. However, the application for insurance against flight delays won the “most innovative blockchain startup award” for its creators, Ehterisc.[⁷](#CH011.html#fn7) The flight delay DApp is described as “a parametric insurance application for consumers,” and is built on the Ehtereum blockchain.

Traditionally, insurance process is tedious, manual, time-consuming and could be frustrating for the policyholder. At the outset, insurance policy application and generation usually require applicant to fill out a detailed form physically or electronically, then submit it to an agent. The application is then evaluated by an underwriter. Once the application is approved the applicant pays the premium and gets the insurance policy. In case of an unfortunate event when the policyholder incurs the damages, she has to submit a claim form to the insurance company. The adjuster employed by the insurance company, verifies the facts of the claim, appraises the losses and eventually pays the policyholder. The entire process of filing a claim and eventually receiving payment from the insurer could take weeks or even months.

The blockchain insurance application autonomously manages the entire business process from application of coverage to payout if needed, and every process in between. The application harnesses the power of smart contracts, as well as different solutions on the blockchain. The App boasted that if a passenger’s flight is delayed by 45 minutes or more, the payout can be received immediately upon landing. The passenger needed to simply check their email and follow simple instructions to get their refund deposited in their bank account. The strategy provides both flexibility and scale that enables new types of insurance products. It has the potential to increase market for insurance products by reducing, or even eliminating, the long wait and uncertainty prevalent with the current method of claim processing.

Axa S.A., a French multinational insurance firm headquartered in Paris and the second largest financial services company in the world, had launched a similar insurance product named Fizzy. It introduced the product in September 2017 as part of a parametric insurance scheme for flight delays. The traditional process for such insurance was for travelers to phone their insurance company, file paperwork, which would then be corroborated with the airline, before the payment was made to the traveler. Conservatively, this process would take a week to a fortnight. Fizzy, instead used publicly available flight statistics and smart contracts to automatically reimburse travelers should their flight be delayed or canceled, usually before the passengers could collect their luggage.

Fizzy too was built on the Ethereum blockchain and smart contracts were formulated upon the passenger purchasing flight insurance. Fizzy was run on a trial basis for flights from Paris to the United States. Its business process was rather simple and was conducted in seven simple steps, fully automated. The steps were as follows:

*   The passenger records the flight.
    
*   The passenger selects the amount of insurance coverage.
    
*   The program then informs passenger of the premium to be paid up-front.
    
*   Passenger pays the premium through their credit card or through cryptocurrency.
    
*   Smart contract is formulated, and the smart contract obtains flight data and delays from publically available databases, such as flight tracker.
    
*   The code of the smart contract determines if there was a delay (two hours or greater).
    
*   When a delay is detected, the smart contract self-executes a simple “if-then” rule and automatically triggers a payment to be deposited in the passenger’s bank account.
    

The above simple product had many advantages over the traditional insurance products and processes. There are no exclusions or uncertainty with the claim processing – in case of delay there is always a payment. The passenger doesn’t even have to file a claim form. The insurance proceeds are deposited to the passenger’s bank account directly upon landing, there are no more trips to the bank to deposit a check. There are no manual intervention from the insurance company whatsoever, reducing the cost of operations for the insurance company.

Unfortunately, two years after its launch, in November 2019 Fizzy was discontinued, as it did not reach the commercial targets. AXA asserted that there was not sufficient market appetite for a blockchain-based consumer product as of that time. However, AXA has committed to continue to test similar insurance products and has gained valuable experience with building and deploying smart contracts by launching Fizzy.

In general blockchain powered decentralized insurance has many advantages that can yet be exploited. Smart contracts enables complete automation, including quoting of the premium, determining when the claim has to be settled, and auto-payments, thus reducing operating costs. Lower costs enable insurance companies to access developing economies and low-income businesses thereby increasing their market reach. Moreover, with reduced costs, insurance companies could offer low margin insurance products such as crop insurance to farmers, etc. The entire contract is transparent, and can be subject to public audit, hence increasing confidence and trust in the industry. As with AXA’s experience with Fizzy, perhaps the society isn’t ready yet but the potential exists and the concept has been tested.

### 11.7. ESTABLISHING PROVENANCE[⁸](#CH011.html#fn8)

With increased consumer literacy and their increased demand for socially responsible behavior on part of the corporations, establishing the provenance of retail products is gaining momentum. For example, when a consumer buys a product that is labeled “Made in U.S.A.,” how do they know what percentage of the raw materials were sourced in the United States. Similarly, when buying Brazilian coffee, how does the consumer know that the coffee beans were indeed grown in Brazil.

Blockchain technology through its ability to enhance traceability, and its property of immutability, allows consumers to ascertain the origin of products or that of its ingredients. The traceability is achieved by entering every detail and process in the manufacturing of the product on a blockchain ledger. Once coded into the blockchain, the record of the product is “permanent, chronically ordered and available to each actor on the network” (Gupta, 2017). This implies that the origin of the product can be traced back to its sources and is permanently stored, thus subject to future scrutiny.

As supply chains become more complex and increasingly global, many organizations contribute through the various stages of the product’s lifecycle. With consumer expectation around transparency increasing as well, there is pressure on large corporations to become more transparent about their supply chain.

The assurance consumers are seeking about a product are of four types: assurance about origin; assurance about authenticity; assurance about custody; and assurance about integrity as illustrated in [Fig. 11.3](#CH011.html#fig3). Blockchain technology can deliver these assurances by providing traceability, certifiability, trackability, immutability and verifiability (Montecchi et al., 2019).

Assurance regarding origin involves identifying whether the product was manufactured under ethical and safe environment without employing child labor with ingredients that were legally sourced from ethical producers. The country of origin of the key ingredients, such as coffee beans or cocoa, could influence consumer opinion and intent to purchase. Authenticity assurance is important for brand name products and luxury goods. For example, when a consumer purchases a Rolex watch or a Prada handbag, they are paying a premium for the brand name and would not like a similar product from a fake manufacturer. Luxury brand manufacturers use various types of trademarks to certify the products’ authenticity, which are typically costly requiring trusted third party. Blockchain technology offers alternatives certifications that are secure and transparent without reliance on intermediaries (Tucker & Catalini, 2018). For expensive products, consumers may demand information on the chain of custody through the transportation and storage phases. Assurance on integrity entails the product’s meeting customers’ expected level of performance and reliability. Blockchain can support this by providing transparency on the supply chain processes, which previously was difficult for consumer to access. This is usually important in exotic fashion products, which claim to use expensive raw materials, like Mongolian cashmere or crocodile leather or hand-woven silk. Providing consumers with a guaranteed assurance on the raw material, integrity of processes and assurance on the proper chain of custody could all be achieved through blockchain.

**Fig. 11.3:    Assurance of Provenance.**

![image](images/C11-FIG3.jpg)

A British company aptly named Provenance,[⁹](#CH011.html#fn9) teamed up with a London-based fashion designer to offer fully traceable garment, where the source of each raw material was recorded and made available to the end consumer. The key raw material was Suri Alpaca fleece which was sourced from a national park. Through the blockchain, it allows the downstream consumer to navigate all the way upstream and be assured of the origin of the fleece.

Another similar example is of an Italian vineyard, Catina Volpone that partnered with EY to develop wine case certified by blockchain technology. This ensures complete transparency of the production process. The end consumer will have access to every minute detail including the dates of harvesting, bottling, etc., and other production details.

Implementing a blockchain solution to the supply chain requires all partners to agree to collaborate and share information. If any of the many parties from the alpaca farmer (as in previous example) to the tanner and the deliverer do not consent to participate, the solution cannot be implemented. Any break in the supply chain would defeat the purpose. Consent from all partners is necessary but not sufficient. The financial commitment from each partner to build and maintain the blockchain is mandatory. Most of the processes will have to be automated to eliminate the possibility of human error. The end customer should be aware and interested in accessing the provenance information and be willing to pay a premium for it to justify all of the upstream costs incurred.

Another area where provenance of the product is valued is in the agricultural supply chain. Blockchain technology can provide a mechanism for immutable record of the product from its harvesting to the retail store, including time-stamp for determination of freshness. Such a system will not only give the consumers trust and confidence in the product they buy but also an opportunity to reward the producers who employ good agricultural practices to cultivate the product. Potentially, this could lead to sustainable farming practices and responsible consumption. Barilla, an Italian manufacturer of pasta sauce had teamed up with IBM to tackle transparency and traceability of its production cycle. The supply chain starts at cultivation, harvest to storage and end at the retail store. For assurance on integrity, it provides detailed quality control information throughout its supply chain. Not only does it ensure food safety, but also it increases brand reputation.

In addition to provenance, the agriculture industry could benefit from insurance, crowd-funding and other similar applications on blockchain. For example, agricultural insurance built through smart contracts could link to key weather data provided by government satellites and make immediate payout in instances of drought or flooding. The process of designing, verifying, implementing and enforcing smart contracts for agricultural insurance is in developmental phase with a few pilot implementations as evidence of proof-of concept. These pilot programs have been implemented in Southeast Asia and the Indian sub-continent. However, despite the benefits, the rate of adoption of these products in the rural and illiterate communities remain low.

Land registration of agricultural land in developing nations is another significant application of blockchain technology. In the impoverished regions of the world, illiteracy compounded with low life expectancy, often lead to misappropriation of land. The United Nations Development Program is working in India to make land registry more reliable. While still at a pilot stage, the social implications of the success of this project cannot be overstated.

### 11.8. CONCLUSION

This chapter provided a brief overview of interesting applications of blockchain technology across many industries, from agriculture to insurance and collateralized borrowing. Blockchain’s key feature is that of providing a platform to enable secure, verified digital transactions in real time. Proper harnessing of this feature would lead to cost savings through efficient operations, simultaneously increasing convenience of the users and their trust in the system and records.

The purpose of this chapter was to demonstrate wide ranging applicability and adoption of this revolutionary technology. Additionally, the purpose was to highlight the opportunities in accounting, banking, finance and taxation that this technology creates. As the technology enables microtransactions, more and more transaction would occur, creating demand for accounting and finance professionals. As can be seen through the examples, each one of these is a demonstration of increased demand for highly skilled accounting and finance professionals to help implement and execute these innovations.
